Online selective conformal inference: adaptive scores, convergence rate and optimality
Humbert, P, Gazin, U, Heller, R, Roquain, E

Abstract
In a supervised online setting, quantifying uncertainty has been proposed in the seminal work of Gibbs and Candes, 2021 . For any given point-prediction algorithm, their method (ACI) produces a conformal prediction set with an average missed coverage getting close to a pre-specified level α \alpha for a long time horizon. We introduce an extended version of this algorithm, called OnlineSCI , allowing the user to additionally select times where such an inference should be made. OnlineSCI encompasses several prominent online selective tasks, such as building prediction intervals for extreme outcomes, classification with abstention, and online testing. While OnlineSCI controls the average missed coverage on the selected in an adversarial setting, our theoretical results also show that it controls the instantaneous error rate (IER) at the selected times, up to a non-asymptotical remainder term. Importantly, our theory covers the case where OnlineSCI updates the point-prediction algorithm at each time step, a property which we refer to as adaptive capability. We show that the adaptive versions of OnlineSCI can convergence to an optimal solution and provide an explicit convergence rate in each of the aforementioned application cases, under specific mild conditions. Finally, the favorable behavior of OnlineSCI in practice is illustrated by numerical experiments.



1
Introduction

The problem of online uncertainty quantification has received much attention in recent years.
The sequential data stream is
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(X_{t},Y_{t}),t\geq 1}}\right\}
, where
X_{t}\in\mathcal{X}
is the covariate and
Y_{t}\in\mathcal{Y}
is the outcome. The goal is typically to produce a prediction set at each time
t
,
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t})
for
Y_{t}
, using the covariate
X_{t}
and all previous information
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(X_{s},Y_{s}),s<t}}\right\}
. We consider two data settings:

1.

Adversarial
:
(X_{t},Y_{t})
,
t\geq 1
, is an arbitrary sequence of random elements in
\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}
, including also the deterministic case.

2.

Identically distributed
: the variables
(X_{t},Y_{t})
,
t\geq 1
, are identically distributed. The prototypical example is the case where these variables are iid. We will also consider the case of a dependent, auto-regressive model.

The adversarial setting is basically a no-assumption setting where at each time
t
, the sequence
(X_{t},Y_{t})
can be chosen arbitrarily. Hence, for any method, the most adversarial sequence is considered. Note that this setting includes as a particular case the situation where the sequence
(X_{t},Y_{t})
,
t\geq 1
, has a (potentially strong) distribution shift in time. By contrast, the identically distributed setting (and in particular the iid setting) is a favorable situation for which we can expect optimality results to hold.

For the adversarial setting,
Gibbs and Candes, 2021
suggested the
adaptive conformal inference
(ACI) method: a gradient descent method that adjusts the width of the prediction set. The sensitivity of ACI to its step-size parameter has led to modifications that tune the step size adaptively
(
Zaffran et al., 2022
;
Gibbs and Candès, 2024
)
.
The aforementioned methods guarantee that the average missed coverage,
\sum^{t}_{s=1}{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{s}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{s}\}}/t
, is in the long run at most a pre-defined target
\alpha
.
However, the instantaneous miss-coverage probability,
\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{{t-1}})
, where
\mathcal{F}_{{t-1}}
denotes the past information at time
t
, may deviate largely from
\alpha
, even if
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(X_{t},Y_{t}),t\geq 1}}\right\}
are independent and identically distributed (iid). Addressing this problem,
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
suggested requiring (in addition to average missed coverage at most
\alpha
) that the algorithm achieves asymptotic non-coverage probability
\alpha
when the online examples are iid. Thus, asymptotically, the coverage guarantee is the same as for a single example in the classic split conformal literature
(
Vovk et al., 2005
)
.
More formally,
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
suggested modifications of ACI that ensure: (1)
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\sum^{t}_{s=1}{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{s}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{s}\}}/t=\alpha
for any adversarial data setting, and (2) in the iid data setting,
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{t})=\alpha
.
Selection occurs when the analyst only wants to report an inference when specific examples of interest show up. For example, when the outcome is expected to be extreme (say extreme whether forecast, where arguably accurate forecast when the weather is not extreme is not of great importance), or the covariates indicate immediate attention is needed (say patients at high risk). A typical setting where selection occurs is when the stream of examples is dense (i.e., new observations arrive frequently), since prediction sets will be reported only at selected time points. With selection, a natural modification is to require that the algorithm satisfies that the non-coverage probability
conditionally on being selected
is asymptotically
\alpha
under the iid assumption for the online examples.
In the conformal inference literature, the non-coverage probability conditional on selection — referred to as the
selection-conditional guarantee
— has been the target of control in the (offline) batch setting studied in
Jin and Ren, 2024
. Specifically, for a test sample consisting of
m
examples,
Jin and Ren, 2024
offer a way to construct prediction sets for the subset of selected examples, guaranteeing that, conditional on selection, the non-coverage probability is at most
\alpha
.
A similar challenge arises in online applications, where it is common for analysts to be interested only in selected time points.

(a)

Standard classic conformal prediction output at
\alpha=0.1
.

(b)

New method
OnlineSCI
.

Figure 1
:
Illustration of the online selective classification application for Cifar10 with classes ‘
cat
’ and ‘
deer
’. The square color indicates whether a classification error occurred: red if it did, green otherwise. When there is no color, this means that we do not make a selection. The classes on top of each selected image corresponds to the prediction set made by the convolutional neural network classifier.

Selection invalidates methods that build on ACI by potentially inflating the false coverage proportion (FCP) among the selected.
This is illustrated in the context of selective classification, where the analyst is interested only in prediction sets of size one. Figure
1(a)
shows that classic conformal prediction sets
(
Vovk et al., 2005
)
, which guarantee
\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{t})=\alpha
, are invalidated by selection. While the FCP is acceptable without selection, it becomes unacceptably high among the selected instances. This is expected, since the instantaneous miss-coverage probability conditional on selection,
\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{t}\mid S_{t}=1)
, can far exceed
\alpha
. On the other hand, Figure
1(b)
shows that with our novel method, the false selection proportion remains at an acceptable level. This is due to the fact that our method abstains from selecting examples that are not trustworthy, and accounts for the selection, as detailed in §
2.3
. Our work adds to the literature on classification with abstention (or with a rejection option), introduced by
Chow, 1970
.
In the machine learning literature, abstention is often accounted for by adding a term to the risk that penalizes any rejection
Herbei and Wegkamp, 2006
;
Bartlett and Wegkamp, 2008
;
Wegkamp and Yuan, 2011
;
Cortes et al., 2016
;
Ni et al., 2019
;
Cao et al., 2022
. Our suggestion, on the other hand, aims to control the miss-classification error rate on the selected, as done in
Zhao and Su, 2023
in the batch setting.

Figure 2
:
Illustration of the online conformal testing application for Cifar10. Classes ‘
plane
’ and ‘
truck
’ correspond to
Y=0
(null) and ‘
cat
’ to
Y=1
(alternative). A red square indicates a false positive and a green square a true positive. No color means no rejection.

In this paper, we introduce novel online selection methods for settings in which the error is revealed after inference on each selected example. Our description is general and encompasses the two main online settings: online testing, where the error corresponds to a false rejection; online interval or set estimation, where the error corresponds to the miscoverage of a selected example. We refer to the proportion of errors among the selected as the FCP, since the inference considered is often that of constructing prediction sets. It reduces to the false discovery proportion (FDP) in online testing.
Our main contributions are as follows:

•

The method, online selective conformal inference, or
OnlineSCI
for short, is introduced in §
2.3
. It is an ACI-type method that accommodates selection, that can be used for making discoveries or providing prediction sets on selected time points. The selection that takes place can be very general. Importantly, the inference for
Y_{t}
can depend on the covariate
X_{t}
as well as on all information accumulated up to the time of the inference, i.e., all the past observations, including when selection occurred and whether an error occurred upon selection.

•

We show in §
2.4
a non-asymptotic FCP
bound, valid in an adversarial setting.

•

The main results are provided in §
3.2
: general convergence rate results for the instantaneous error rate (IER) of
OnlineSCI
, both with high probability (Theorem
3.2
) or in expectation (Theorem
3.3
). These results come from general non-asymptotical approximation bounds provided in §
A
. The convergence rate is shown to depend on two key ingredients: the way the predictive algorithm is stabilized near a benchmark a time
t
(measured via a distance
D_{t}
) and the way the selection number (
J(t)
) at time
t
does concentrate.
This leads in particular to practical guidelines for choosing the decaying step size of
OnlineSCI
.

•

The main results are applied for various selection rules which are
X
-oriented (i.e., covariate-oriented) in §
4
, especially for the iid regression model (§
4.2
and §
4.4
), including an optimality result in §
4.3
, and for the stationary auto-regressive model in §
4.5
. In each setting, we provide a specific convergence rate to the oracle which explicitly depends on the estimation quality of the regression mean (and variance).

•

The main results are applied for decision-oriented, informative selections in §
5
, for which the selection at time
t
depends on the threshold of
OnlineSCI
at time
t
: selective classification (§
5.2
) where prediction sets are constrained to be of size one, and online conformal testing (§
5.3
) where selecting is rejecting the null hypotheses. As a byproduct, we provide an online method for selection by prediction (§
5.4
), a problem introduced considered by
Jin and Candes, 2023
in the batch setting.
Similarly to the previous section, a convergence rate to the optimal solution is provided, but with the difference that the remainder terms contain an (unavoidable) error term
\delta>0
, not tending to zero when
t
grows, although being small in practice.

•

Numerical illustrations are provided in §
6
. They support the theoretical findings by showing that the FCP and IER of
OnlineSCI
are converging to the nominal value
\alpha
, contrary to the ACI method of
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
which is impacted by the selection effect. Also, it puts forward that the adaptation stage (i.e., estimating the score or test statistic) in
OnlineSCI
is important to converge to the optimal procedure. We illustrate that using a non-adaptive score in
OnlineSCI
can lead to much lower power than using an adaptive score.

Next we introduce briefly two of our main applications. Then, we conclude the introduction by relating our goals and the terminology we use to that in previous literature.

1.1
Online conformal testing

In the statistical literature, online testing focuses
on inference over an infinite sequence of fixed, unknown parameters ordered in time, see
Javanmard and Montanari, 2018
and references within.
Javanmard and Montanari, 2018
proposed methods that control the expected false discovery proportion. Our setting assumes that the FDP up to (and including) time
t
is known at time
t+1
, enabling a more direct approach. Specifically, we propose a strategy, detailed in Algorithm
1
, that explicitly targets the local false discovery rate
(
Efron et al., 2001
)
. At time
t
the binary variable
Y_{t}\in\{0,1\}
denotes the hypothesis state, where
Y_{t}=0
if the null hypothesis is true, and
Y_{t}=1
otherwise. See
Jin and Candès, 2023
for interesting applications in which null hypotheses on outcomes are considered.
Let
S_{t}\in\{0,1\}
denote the test-driven selection rule that depends on the covariate
X_{t}
and all previous information
\{(X_{s},Y_{s}),s<t\}
. Among the selected, the aim is to have a small false discovery proportion,

\mathrm{FDP}_{t}:=\frac{\sum_{s=1}^{t}S_{s}\cdot{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{s}=0\}}}{\sum_{s=1}^{t}S_{s}},

as well as good discovery power. The oracle test statistic is the
local false discovery rate
(lfdr), which is defined at time
t
as
\mathrm{lfdr}_{t}:=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}=0\mid X_{t})
. Since the optimal policy in the iid case is to threshold the local false discovery rate
(
Sun and Cai, 2007
)
, the test-driven selection rule suggested in Algorithm
1
is based on thresholding the estimated lfdr at each time step.
Hence, in a nutshell, our conformal testing procedure can be seen as an online counterpart of the Sun and Cai procedure
(
Sun and Cai, 2007
)
.
Our theoretical results in §
2.4
shows that
\mathrm{FDP}_{t}
converges to be at most the nominal level
\alpha
. In §
5.3
, we show that moreover if the online examples are iid, under mild assumptions, the probability of a true rejection at time
t
converges to the optimal probability to discover a false null hypothesis, while guaranteeing that the probability that the null hypothesis is true conditional on being selected is at most
\alpha
. Thus, we not only provide a meaningful guarantee of a small asymptotic false discovery proportion in all adversarial settings, but also achieve asymptotically optimal instantaneous decisions when the online examples happen to be drawn from the same mixture distribution. We also provide explicit convergence rates, see §
5.3
.

We expect Algorithm
1
to be particularly useful for online novelty detection. In novelty detection, a test is carried out in order to rule out that the new observation is sampled from the same (unknown) distribution of a null sample. It is also referred to as out-of-distribution testing or outlier detection
(
Blanchard et al., 2010
;
Liang et al., 2022
;
Pimentel et al., 2014
)
. In this setting, at time
t
the binary variable
Y_{t}\in\{0,1\}
codes for an inlier whenever
Y_{t}=0
or an outlier/novelty whenever
Y_{t}=1
.
Figure
2
illustrates an application to novelty detection, where the null hypothesis is that the image is not novel (i.e.,
Y_{t}=0
), and the goal is to detect as many novelties as possible (i.e.,
Y_{t}=1
). For this particular example, there are no discoveries by the LORD algorithm suggested in
Javanmard and Montanari, 2018
. This is not surprising, since access to the outcomes
(Y_{t})_{t\geq 1}
enables more powerful inference while maintaining a low proportion of false discoveries among the selected.

Input :
Targeted level
\alpha\in(0,1)
; step size
(\gamma_{t})_{t\geq 1}
; initial threshold
q_{1}
; initial labeled training data
\mathcal{D}_{0}
; an algorithm that takes as input some training labeled data and the covariates of a new example, and returns the estimated
\mathrm{lfdr}
,
\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}
, for the new example.

J\leftarrow 0
;

1

\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}_{0}
;

for

t\geq 1

do

2

Observe
X_{t}
;

3

\mathrm{lfdr}_{t}\leftarrow\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}(X_{t};\mathcal{D})
(estimated lfdr for the example at time
t
);

4

if

\mathrm{lfdr}_{t}\leq 1-q_{t}

then

5

S_{t}\leftarrow 1
(reject “
Y_{t}=0
’’);

6

J\leftarrow J+1
;

7

Observe
Y_{t}
;

8

if

q_{t}\geq 0

and

Y_{t}=1

then

9

q_{t+1}\leftarrow q_{t}-\gamma_{J}\alpha
;

10

end if

11

else

12

q_{t+1}\leftarrow q_{t}+\gamma_{J}(1-\alpha)
;

13

end if

14

end if

15

else

16

S_{t}\leftarrow 0
(accept “
Y_{t}=0
’’);

17

\mathcal{D}\leftarrow\mathcal{D}\cup\{(X_{t},Y_{t})\}
;

18

end if

19

end for

20

Output :
At each time
t
,
S_{t}
the rejection indicator.

Algorithm 1

Online conformal testing with local false discovery rate

1.2
Online selective interval estimation

For online selective interval estimation,
(
Weinstein and Ramdas, 2020
)
suggested controlling the expected false coverage proportion. The case that
(Y_{s})_{s<t}
is observed
has only recently been considered by
Bao et al., 2024a
;
Sale and Ramdas, 2025
.
As in online testing, let
S_{t}\in\{0,1\}
denote an arbitrary selection rule that may depend on
the covariate
X_{t}
and on all previous information
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(X_{s},Y_{s}),s<t}}\right\}
.
A simple example of a selection rule is, for instance,
S_{t}(x)={\mathbf{1}\{x\in{\mathcal{A}}\}}
where
{\mathcal{A}}\subset\mathcal{X}
is a (measurable) subset of interest for the covariate
x

(
Bao et al., 2024a
)
. In other words, with this particular selection rule, we only want to report
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}
when
X_{t}
is in
{\mathcal{A}}
.
Among the selected, criterion (1) of
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
becomes the false coverage proportion (FCP) on the selected times at most
t
, defined as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{FCP}_{t}({\mathcal{R}}):=\dfrac{\sum^{t}_{s=1}S_{s}\cdot{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{s}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{s}\}}}{1\vee\sum^{t}_{s=1}S_{s}}\;,

(1)

for procedure
{\mathcal{R}}=((S_{t})_{t\geq 1},({\mathcal{C}}_{t})_{t\geq 1})
, defined both by
a selection rule and prediction sets
, where
a\vee b=\max\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{a,b}}\right\}
for any
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
.
As with selective classification, by constructing prediction sets using ACI or a variant of ACI and reporting them only for selected examples, we may have
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}({\mathcal{R}})\gg\alpha\ \forall t
even in the iid setting; see
Bao et al., 2024a
and §
3
.

Bao et al., 2024a
suggested a simple fix to ACI for asymptotic
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}
control and considered a novel procedure if the data are iid. They did not consider bounding the instantaneous miss-coverage probability among the selected, which is the equivalent of criterion (2) in
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
when selection takes place.

1.3
Related work and terminology

Online conformal inference refers to ACI type procedures, i.e., gradient descent algorithms with the pinball loss. The method we shall suggest is also of the ACI type, but we adjust for the fact that inference is carried out only at selected time points. Therefore we refer to our method as
online selective conformal inference
,
OnlineSCI
for short.
We thus propose an
online selective classification
method to complement existing ACI-type procedures for classification without selection, and
an online selective regression
method to complement those for regression.

ACI-type methods are adaptive to covariate shifts by construction. This is achieved by updating the empirical quantile over time using the gradient descent algorithm. In this work, we say that the (ACI-type) method is
adaptive
if it updates over time the non-conformity score, and more generally the inference. So an adaptive
OnlineSCI
will use a selection rule
S_{t}
that depends on
X_{t}
and on
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
(all data before time
t
).

Bao et al., 2024a
;
Sale and Ramdas, 2025
are two recent works that consider the setting of online selective conformal inference for the purpose of constructing prediction sets. Specifically, they consider controlling the probability of non-coverage conditional on being selected,
\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{t}\}}\>|\>S_{t}=1]
, in the iid online setting. In this work, we shall be more adaptive, using all past information in order to improve the score and the selection rule, so we shall aim to control instead
\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}\notin{\mathcal{C}}_{t}\}}\>|\>S_{t}=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
.

In the multiple testing literature, it is standard to aim for control of the probability of a false positive conditional on rejection,
\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}=0\}}\>|\>S_{t}=1]
, where
Y_{t}=0
indicates the selected example comes from a null hypothesis. This is under the assumption that the test statistics are generated from the two-groups model
(
Efron, 2003
;
Storey, 2003
)
.
We refer to
OnlineSCI
in the setting of testing as
online conformal testing
. The error we shall be interested in is
\mathbb{E}[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}=0\}}\>|\>S_{t}=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]
. If the examples are iid, it is possible to converge to the optimal decision rule which is
well understood for the two group model
Sun and Cai, 2007
;
Heller and Rosset, 2020
, see §
5
.

One innovation of this manuscript is to derive explicit rates for IER convergence of
OnlineSCI
in the context of selection.
For a different purpose,
Labopin-Richard et al., 2019
also derived rates for a gradient descent type algorithm for which a selection stage is performed: the aim was to build an online approximation of the quantile of the distribution of the score conditionally on
X=x_{0}
, by selecting at time
t
only if
X_{t}
is in a neighborhood of
x_{0}
(with some specific vanishing size). While it shares some similarities with our work, the problem therein is to obtain an asymptotical conditional coverage, so is markedly different than ours where the selection is driven by the user (although we can also recover the conditional coverage with optimality in the specific iid online regression model, see §
4.3
). The algorithm is also slightly different, as they update the step sequence at each time step, even when no selection is made at the previous time.

2
Preliminaries

In this section, we introduce the setting and notation, the method
OnlineSCI
and the bound on the false error proportion.

2.1
Setting

To model the information that is available when the time goes forward, we introduce the filtration

\mathcal{F}_{t}:=\sigma({\mathcal{D}}_{0},\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{(X_{k},Y_{k}),1\leq k\leq t}}\right\}),\quad t\geq 1,

(2)

where
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}:=(X_{i},Y_{i})_{-(n-1)\leq i\leq 0}
is an holdout set of size
n
available at time
1
.
Hence,
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
represents information available at time
t
, before observing
X_{t}
.

A procedure
{\mathcal{R}}=((S_{t})_{t\geq 1},({\mathcal{C}}_{t})_{t\geq 1})
is any sequence such that, at each time
t\geq 1
, it comprises a selection decision
S_{t}\in\{0,1\}
and a prediction set
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}\subset\mathcal{Y}
, both measurable
1
1

1

For
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}
, the measurability of
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}
has to be taken in the following sense: for all
t\geq 1
, the set
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}
is any function of
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
and
X_{t}
such that the set
\{Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}}_{t}\}
is measurable.

with respect to
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
and
X_{t}
.

Throughout the paper, we focus on a particular class of procedures that are classically used in online CP
(
Gibbs and Candes, 2021
;
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
)
and are formally introduced as follows.

Definition 1
.

A threshold-sequence based procedure is a triple

{\mathcal{R}}=((S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}))_{t\geq 1},({\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}))_{t\geq 1},(q_{t})_{t\geq 1}),

where

•

for all
t\geq 1
:
each threshold
q_{t}\in\mathbb{R}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable; each selection rule
S_{t}:(x,q)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto S_{t}(x,q)\in\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{0,1}}\right\}
is such that
S_{t}(x,q)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable; each prediction set rule
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}:(x,q)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(x,q)\in\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
is such that
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(x,q)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable.

•

For some
B>0
, at least one of the following must apply for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
t\geq 1
, and
q\geq B
: the prediction set is the entire space,
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(x,q)=\mathcal{Y}
; or there is no selection,
S_{t}(x,q)=0
.

The prediction set rule at time
t
is typically of one of the two following forms: for
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
q<B
,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(x,q)

\displaystyle:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{y\in\mathcal{Y}\;:\;V_{t}(x,y)\leq q}}\right\}\;;

(3)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(x,q)

\displaystyle:=\arg\min_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\{V_{t}(x,y)\}\;,

(4)

where
V_{t}:(x,y)\in\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y}\mapsto V_{t}(x,y)\in[0,B]

is a given
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable non-conformity score function which is large when
y
is far from its point-prediction at
x
(and such that
\arg\min_{y\in\mathcal{Y}}\{V_{t}(x,y)\}\neq\emptyset
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
2
2

2

We assume here that the argmin is a singleton, otherwise we choose
\{\min\mathcal{Y}\}
by convention.
).

The selection rule
S_{t}(\cdot,\cdot)
at time
t
can be arbitrarily general. We divide them into two main types:

•

A rule that does not depend on the threshold decision
q
, i.e.,
S_{t}(x,q)=S_{t}(x).
The rule can depend on
t
and on
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
. Such rules have been considered in
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
;
Bao et al., 2024a
;
Sale and Ramdas, 2025
. Here, they are referred to as
X
-oriented selection rules.

•

A rule that depends on the threshold decision
q
, so it is typically of the following form

\displaystyle S_{t}(x,q)={\mathbf{1}\{W_{t}(x)>q\}},\>\>\>q\in\mathbb{R},

(5)

where
W_{t}:x\in\mathcal{X}\mapsto W_{t}(x)\in[0,B]

is a given
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable ‘test’ statistics. Note that the rule (
5
) always selects when
q<0
.
A selection rule that depends on
q
may select based on the inference made. We refer to such a rule as an
informative selection rule
, by following the terminology of
Gazin et al., 2024
in the batch setting. It takes in general the form of a constraint on the produced prediction set
\mathcal{C}_{t}
(e.g., size, excluding elements) if selected. For demonstration, we will focus the constraints
|\mathcal{C}_{t}|=1
(selective classification) or
0\notin\mathcal{C}_{t}
(conformal testing), each case corresponding to a specific choice for the test statistic
W_{t}(x)
.

We briefly summarize prediction set rules and selection rules for several applications for which we provide novel online procedures with error guarantees. The detailed algorithms for these applications are provided in §
D
.

Application 1: online selective conformal prediction intervals.

In (two-sided) prediction intervals for regression
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbb{R}
, we can choose the score function to be the rescaled residual
(
Papadopoulos et al., 2008
;
Papadopoulos et al., 2011
)
: for
x\in\mathcal{X}
,
y\in\mathcal{Y}
,

V_{t}(x,y)=2B\Phi\Big(|y-\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)|/\hat{\sigma}_{t}(x)\Big)-B\in(0,B)

(6)

for some estimators
\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)
(resp.
\hat{\sigma}_{t}^{2}(x)
) of the conditional expectation (resp. variance) of
Y_{t}
given
X_{t}=x
(each estimator being
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
measurable).
Note that the residual is rescaled in (
6
) to be valued in
[0,B]
, which is a requirement for the score
(
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
)
. The rescaling is done here by the function
2B\Phi(\cdot)-B
, where
\Phi
is the c.d.f. of the standard normal distribution, although this choice is arbitrary and not important for the sequel.
In this case, (
3
) reduces to the prediction interval

\mathcal{C}_{t}(x,q)=\Big[\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)\pm\hat{\sigma}_{t}(x)\Phi^{-1}\Big(\frac{q+B}{2B}\Big)\Big],

(7)

if
q\in(0,B)
, with
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x,q)=\{\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)\}
if
q\leq 0
and
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x,q)=\mathbb{R}
if
q\geq B
.
This prediction set will be considered with an
X
-oriented selection, see §
4
.

Application 2: online selective conformal classification.

In selective classification
\mathcal{Y}=[K]
. The non-conformity score
V_{t}(x,y)
is usually an estimator
\hat{}\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\neq y\mid X_{t}=x)
of the probability of
Y_{t}\neq y
given
X_{t}=x
at time
t
(the estimator being
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
measurable). Since it is generally done after a softmax step, we often have
V_{t}(x,y)\in(0,1)
.
In that situation, the prediction set is given by (
4
).
The selection rule uses
W_{t}(x)=\max_{y\in[K]}(1-V_{t}(x,y))
, which corresponds to selecting the examples for which the estimated probability of being in a class is large enough.

Application 3: online conformal testing.

For online conformal testing (e.g., novelty detection)
\mathcal{Y}=\{0,1\}
. The binary variable
Y_{t}\in\{0,1\}
codes for a true null hypothesis whenever
Y_{t}=0
or a false null hypothesis whenever
Y_{t}=1
. We choose
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x,q)=\{1\}
for all
x,q
. For the selection rule,
W_{t}(x)
measures the inadequacy between the null examples (an inlier in novelty detection) and the observed
x
using, for instance, a one-class classifier
(
Bates et al., 2023
)
(only based on inliers), or a two-class classifier
(
Liang et al., 2022
;
Marandon et al., 2024
)
(based both on inliers and outliers).

To construct a threshold-sequence based procedure, we need to properly define the sequence
(q_{t})_{t\geq 1}
. We would like
(q_{t})_{t\geq 1}
to be such that there are not too many errors among the selected, yet many inferences of interest are made. We define two important notions of error in the next subsections, and then show our suggestion for
(q_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and its theoretical guarantees in the next section.

Remark 2.1
.

In our theory, the prediction set is let free to the user when
q<0
. This was not allowed in
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
for which the prediction set was forced to be empty in that situation.

2.2
Error rates

To evaluate the performance of a given procedure
{\mathcal{R}}=((S_{t})_{t\geq 1},({\mathcal{C}}_{t})_{t\geq 1})
, we measure whether the decision taken at time
t
for the response
y
is an error or not with an
error function
:

\mathrm{err}_{t}:(y,C)\in\mathcal{Y}\times\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})\mapsto\mathrm{err}_{t}(y,C)\in[0,1]\,,

(8)

with the conventions
\mathrm{err}_{t}(y,\mathcal{Y})=0
and
\mathrm{err}_{t}(y,\emptyset)=1
for all
y\in\mathcal{Y}
, and where
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{Y})
denotes the subsets of
\mathcal{Y}
.
While the most classical choice is the coverage error

\mathrm{err}_{t}(y,C)={\mathbf{1}\{y\notin C\}}

(9)

as in
(
Gibbs and Candes, 2021
)
, other choices are covered by our theory, see Remark
2.2
. Given this error function, the average error rate over past selection of the procedure
{\mathcal{R}}=((S_{t})_{t\geq 1},({\mathcal{C}}_{t})_{t\geq 1})
, referred to as the FCP, is defined
3
3

3

Note that we implicitly assume here that
\mathrm{err}_{k}(Y_{k},{\mathcal{C}}_{k})
is measurable.
as follows at time
t
:

\displaystyle\qquad\mathrm{FCP}_{t}({\mathcal{R}})

\displaystyle:=\dfrac{\sum_{k=1}^{t}\mathrm{err}_{k}(Y_{k},{\mathcal{C}}_{k})\cdot S_{k}}{1\vee\sum_{k=1}^{t}S_{k}}\,.

(10)

If in addition
{\mathcal{C}}_{k}\equiv\{1\}
and
\mathcal{Y}=\{0,1\}
,
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}({\mathcal{R}})=\big(\sum_{k=1}^{t}{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{k}=0\}}\cdot S_{k}\big)/\big(1\vee\sum_{k=1}^{t}S_{k}\big)
corresponds to the FDP.

Remark 2.2
.

Our work can accommodate non-symmetric errors. For instance, in classification, we may consider
\mathrm{err}_{t}(y,C)=\sum_{k\in[K]}w_{k}{\mathbf{1}\{y=k,k\notin C\}}
, for some weights
w_{k}\geq 0
with
\sum_{k\in[K]}w_{k}=1
. In regression, we can consider a more general error
\mathrm{err}_{t}(x,C)\in[0,1]
measuring the distance
between
y
and the set
C
.

Algorithms that guarantee that the average error over the selected is at most
\alpha
may nevertheless be unsatisfactory, since we can have average non-coverage at most
\alpha
even if the instantaneous non-coverage probability at each time step is either much larger, or much smaller, than
\alpha
. If it is smaller than
\alpha
, the prediction set is too wide (and hence less accurate); if it is larger than
\alpha
then the non-coverage probability may be unacceptably high (since the prediction set is too narrow).

This work aims to control the probability that the output prediction set makes an error, conditional on being selected.
We refer to this quantity as the
instantaneous error rate
at time
t\geq 1
for a given procedure
{\mathcal{R}}=((S_{t})_{t\geq 1},({\mathcal{C}}_{t})_{t\geq 1})
, and define it as:

\displaystyle\mathrm{IER}_{t}({\mathcal{R}}):=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{err}_{t}(Y_{t},{\mathcal{C}}_{t})\>|\>S_{t}=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}].

(11)

2.3
OnlineSCI

We introduce our method, called
OnlineSCI
.

Definition 2
.

The
OnlineSCI
procedure with selection rules
(S_{t}(\cdot,\cdot))_{t\geq 1}
and prediction set rules
({\mathcal{C}}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot))_{t\geq 1}
is the procedure
{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}=(S_{t}(X_{t},q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}),{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}),q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
as in Definition
1
, where the threshold sequence
(q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
is given by the following recursion:

\displaystyle q_{t+1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}

\displaystyle=q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}+\gamma_{J(t)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{react}_{t}(Y_{t},{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}),q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha}}\right)\cdot S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})\;,

(12)

where
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B)
,
(\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
is a positive sequence of real numbers,
J(t)
given by

J(t):=\sum_{i=1}^{t-1}S_{i}(X_{i},q_{i}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})+1

(13)

is the number of selections before time
t
(plus one), and the function
\mathrm{react}_{t}(\cdot,\cdot,\cdot)
is given by:

\mathrm{react}_{t}(y,C,q):=\mathrm{err}_{t}(y,C)\cdot{\mathbf{1}\{q\geq 0\}}+{\mathbf{1}\{q<0\}}\;,

(14)

with
\mathrm{err}_{t}(\cdot)
defined by (
8
).

In words, the recursion (
12
) works by updating
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}
only if there is a selection, that is,
S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})=1
, which leads to a piece-wise constant sequence
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}
, whose value is updated only at each selection time. A decision
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})
is produced at the selected time and the value of
Y_{t}
is revealed. Then, the update increases the value of
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}
by
\gamma_{J(t)}(1-\alpha)
when there is an error (or if
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}<0
) and decreases the value by
\gamma_{J(t)}\alpha
otherwise. Importantly, the step
\gamma_{J(t)}
depends on the selection number
J(t)
, which means that it is also updated only at each selection. Note also that
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}
is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable and thus
OnlineSCI
is a properly defined threshold-sequence based procedure (see Definition
1
).

OnlineSCI
is an extension of ACI
(
Gibbs and Candes, 2021
)
that presents several innovations. First, it follows the approach of
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
, by using a step sequence
(\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
that tends to zero.
As proved therein in case of no selective inference, this entails an interesting convergence behavior of
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}
(in the iid case), which limits the oscillations of the original ACI threshold sequence (that used a constant
(\gamma_{j})_{j}
sequence), in particular reducing drastically the occurrences of the threshold below
0
and above
B
(as we discuss below). Second,
OnlineSCI
allows for incorporating a selection rule by updating the threshold only at the selected time. Doing so, it inherits the classical FCP control of
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
in restriction to the selection, as we formally prove in §
2.4
. Third, its formulation is flexible enough to accommodate several settings, as those of our application cases (in particular the online conformal testing one). Hence, the same core recursion (
12
) can be adapted into several variants, see Algorithms
1
,
2
and
3
. In addition, results for each of these variants can be obtained by establishing general properties of
OnlineSCI
(results in §
3.2
provide corollaries in §
4
and §
5
).

The procedure
OnlineSCI
has a specific behavior when
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}
is outside
[0,B]
.
First, if
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}<0
, the reaction function forces
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t+1}
to increase
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}
, whatever being the rule
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q)
for
q<0
. As a consequence, the threshold sequence
(q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t})_{t}
does not depend on the choice of
\mathcal{C}_{t}(X_{t},q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t})
when
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}<0
, so the analyst is free to choose what to do in this setting.
This is different from the method of
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
, that imposes
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x,q)=\emptyset
for
q<0
(hence imposing an error each time
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}<0
).
Choosing
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x,q)\neq\emptyset
can only decrease the FCP value so is in general desirable, if it makes sense in the specific setting considered.
For regression, we suggest providing a point prediction when
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}<0
. For selective classification and conformal testing, we suggest
4
4

4

This is suggested for completeness, but this case corresponds to a ’favorable’ situation where many errors have not been made in a row, and it is rarely met in practice.
that the decision on whether to create a non-empty prediction set, and what it should contain, be made based on the available evidence regardless of the value of
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}
.

Second, if
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}\geq B
, according to Definition
1
, we should have either
\mathcal{C}_{t}(X_{t},q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t})=\mathcal{Y}
(e.g., in the case of regression see (
3
)) or
S_{t}(X_{t},q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t})=0
(e.g., in the case of informative selection (
5
)). In the case where
S_{t}(x,q)=S_{t}(x)
does not depend on
q
, we are in the former case and
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t+1}
decrease below
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}
until going below
B
, and the algorithm goes on until it converges to the appropriate quantity (as we show in §
4
, in which it is proven that
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}\geq B
occurs with a probability vanishing when
t
grows). In the case where the selection is of the form (
5
), we are in the latter case and
S_{t}(X_{t},q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t})=0
, which entails
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{T}=q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}
for all
T>t
. Hence, the algorithm ’is frozen’ above
B
so cannot converge to the appropriate quantity. We provide below comments about this fact:

•

this behavior is not a problem specific to
OnlineSCI
: it is unavoidable to get an adversarial FCP bound and a non-trivial prediction set, see Proposition
C.1
;

•

the probability that
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}\geq B
is empirically small, but non-zero, and theoretically quantified by the term
\delta
(
49
) in §
5
.

In practice, this is not limitation: when
q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t_{0}}\geq B
for a selection of the form (
5
), we suggest to restart SCI, using all the information up to
t_{0}
in order to adjust the starting value (
q_{1}
) and the first step sequence (
\gamma_{1}
), and even the method for building prediction sets and the selection rule to make a less stringent selection.
All our results apply with the new holdout set
\mathcal{D}_{0}:=\mathcal{F}_{t_{0}}
of size
n:=n+t_{0}
.

2.4
FDP bound

Following the argument in
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
restricted on the selection, we show the procedure
OnlineSCI
controls the
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}
(
10
) in the adversarial setting. We first show that the sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
returned by
OnlineSCI
is always bounded.

Lemma 2.1
.

For all
t\geq 1
and any sequence
(X_{t},Y_{t})_{t\geq 1}
the sequence of thresholds
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
of
OnlineSCI
is such that
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[-\alpha\max_{1\leq j\leq J(t)}(\gamma_{j}),B+(1-\alpha)\max_{1\leq j\leq J(t)}(\gamma_{j})]
.

Theorem 2.2
.

For any sequence
(X_{t},Y_{t})_{t\geq 1}
, the procedure
OnlineSCI
is such that
for all
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{FCP}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha+\dfrac{B+\max_{1\leq j\leq J(t)}(\gamma_{j})}{J(t)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma^{-1}_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{J(t)}\lvert\gamma^{-1}_{j}-\gamma^{-1}_{j-1}\rvert}}\right)\;,

(15)

where recall that
J(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})+1
, see (
13
). If we further assume that the sequence of
(\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
is nonincreasing, we have:

\displaystyle\mathrm{FCP}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})

\displaystyle\leq\alpha+\frac{B+\gamma_{1}}{J(t)\gamma_{J(t)}}\;.

(16)

The proofs of the two previous results can be found in §
B.1
.
The bound (
15
) provides an upper bound on the
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}
and shares similarities with the previous bound of
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
. However, there are two main differences: first, our algorithm includes a selection part, which makes the problem more challenging and slows down the convergence of the upper-bound if there are only few selections. Second, as we always increase
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}
when it is negative (reason why we use
\mathrm{react}_{t}
and not just
\mathrm{err}_{t}
), the situation where
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}=0
can occur and no positive lower bound for
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}
can be provided. This behavior is more natural, for example, in the situation where we can perfectly predict
Y_{t}
. Indeed, in this situation, we can make no error, but previous online CP algorithms based on ACI are forced to make unnecessary errors so that
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}
tends to
\alpha
. Finally, a similar bounds on the FCP in the selective case has also been proposed by
Bao et al., 2024a
for another variant of ACI.

Remark 2.3
.

In the conformal testing example, recall that
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}=\mathrm{FDP}_{t}
. The bound (
15
) is thus completely new to our knowledge and says that the
\mathrm{FDP}_{t}
is bounded close to
\alpha
if enough novelties have been made in time before time
t
, even in an adversarial setting. This is in sharp contrast with the previous literature for which the distribution of an alternative is by definition the same across time, see §
1.1
.

3
Convergence rates

This section presents approximation results for the
OnlineSCI
procedure. For short, the explicit finite-sample approximation bounds are deferred to §
A
and we focus here on the resulting convergence rates.

3.1
Benchmark IER function

First note that the instantaneous error rate (
11
) of
OnlineSCI
is given by

\displaystyle\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})=\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}),\>\>t\geq 1,

(17)

for
q_{t}\in[0,B]
,
for the functional

\displaystyle\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}:q\in[0,B]\mapsto\>

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{err}_{t}(Y_{t},{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q))\>|\>S_{t}(X_{t},q)=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}],

(18)

with
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}(q)=0
by convention if
\mathbb{P}(S_{t}(X_{t},q)=1\>|\>\mathcal{F}_{t-1})=0
. It corresponds, at each time step, to the probability that the prediction set makes an error conditionally on being selected (and on the past).
When
X_{t}
is independent of the past
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
, the integration in (
18
) is made only wrt the distribution of
(X_{t},Y_{t})
. Under dependence, however, the distribution of
(X_{t},Y_{t})
should be taken conditionally on
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
.
Note that
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}(q)
is not defined for
q<0
. Remember that
\mathcal{C}_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})
is let arbitrary for
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}<0
, so we do not intend to find an IER bound in that case (which rarely occurs anyway, see §
2.3
).

The error function
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}
can take various forms in specific cases, and this will be explored in depth in Sections
4
and
5
. To provide already some intuition here, consider the probably easiest case of a time-constant
X
-oriented selection
S_{t}(x,q)=S(x)
and of a regression model (see (
31
) below) with true conditional mean and variance functions
\mu(x)
and
\sigma^{2}(x)
, respectively. Then using the adaptive score (
6
) with estimator
\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)
and
\hat{\sigma}^{2}_{t}(x)
depending on the past
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
gives

\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}(q)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|Y_{t}-\hat{\mu}_{t}(X_{t})|/\hat{\sigma}_{t}(X_{t})>\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q+B}{2B}}}\right)\mid S(X_{t})=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}}\right),\>\>q\in[0,B].

(19)

If the estimators have suitable estimation properties,
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}
should get close to the function
\Pi^{0}(q)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|Y-{\mu}(X)|/{\sigma}(X)>\Phi^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{q+B}{2B}}}\right)\mid S(X)=1}}\right)
, that we interpret here a benchmark error function. Under mild assumptions, this function
\Pi^{0}
is decreasing continuous on
[0,B]
and thus solving
\Pi^{0}(q)=\alpha
provides a natural candidate
q^{0}_{\alpha}
for the limit of
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}
.

More generally, we assume in our theory that
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}
gets close to a benchmark function
\Pi^{0}
and the distance between the two is measured with

D_{t}:=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\Pi^{0}(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})}}\right\rvert{\mathbf{1}\{q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}\in[0,B]\}}\leq\|\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}-\Pi^{0}\|_{\infty},\>\>t\geq 1.

(20)

Note that
D_{t}
is a
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable random variable. When
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}
is based upon estimators,
D_{t}
is small if the estimation quality is high. Also note that we can choose to have
D_{t}=0
if
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}
is a deterministic function not depending on
t
, because we may take
\Pi^{0}:=\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}
. However, the latter is generally not a good choice because it is not adaptive: the prediction rule cannot be updated in time so that
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}
will converge to a suboptimal solution.
For instance in (
19
),
D_{t}=0
occurs if the estimators
\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)
,
\hat{\sigma}_{t}(x)
are based upon a different independent sample so that they can be considered as fixed and taking
\Pi^{0}:=\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}
is possible because it does not depend anymore on
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
. This however comes to price the that
\Pi^{0}
does not rely on the true
\mu(\cdot)
and
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)
.
By contrast,
D_{t}
is positive (and random) if these estimator are based upon
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
, while we can target the true
\mu(\cdot)
and
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)
in
\Pi^{0}
(as in the above expression).
In what follows the case
D_{t}=0
is referred to as the
non-adaptive
case, while using
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}
incorporating some
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable estimates is referred to as the
adaptive
case.

We will make the following regularity assumption:

Assumption 1
.

The benchmark function
\Pi^{0}:q\in[0,B]\mapsto\Pi^{0}(q)\in[0,1]
is a (deterministic) function with
\Pi^{0}(q^{0}_{\alpha})=\alpha
for some
q^{0}_{\alpha}\in(0,B)
. In addition,
\Pi^{0}

is nonincreasing on
[0,B]
and continuously differentiable on some compact
[\ell_{\alpha},u_{\alpha}]\subset(0,1)
with
\ell_{\alpha}<q^{0}_{\alpha}<u_{\alpha}
and
(\Pi^{0})^{\prime}(q^{0}_{\alpha})>0
.

As we will see further on, this assumption is very mild and will be satisfied in our application cases (up to reduce the
\alpha
range in some cases).
It can also be relaxed (in particular the global non-increasingness can be dropped) and we refer the interested reader to §
A
.

3.2
Main results

We start by recalling the following classical conditions (see, e.g.,
Chen, 2002
) on the step sequence of the Robbins Monro algorithm:

\sum_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}=\infty\>\>\mbox{ and }\>\>\sum_{j\geq 1}\gamma^{2}_{j}<\infty.

(21)

The following result holds (see §
B.3
for a proof).

Theorem 3.1
.

[Strong consistency]
Consider the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
with a step sequence
(\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
satisfying (
21
) and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
.
Consider some function
\Pi^{0}
satisfying Assumption
1
with some
q^{0}_{\alpha}\in(0,B)
and the corresponding
D_{t}
being defined by (
20
). Recall that
J(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})+1
is given by (
13
).
Then, almost surely on the event
\{\lim_{t\to\infty}J(t)=+\infty\}\cap\{\lim_{t\to\infty}D_{t}=0\}
, we have

\lim_{t\to\infty}q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}=q^{0}_{\alpha}.

Theorem
3.1
is well known in the case of no selective inference, with the slightly relaxed assumption that
\Pi^{0}
is continuous and only crosses
\alpha
in
q^{0}_{\alpha}
with
\Pi^{0}(0)>\alpha
and
\Pi^{0}(B)<\alpha

(
Chen, 2002
)
5
5

5

In light of our proof and our relaxed assumption Assumption
7
, Theorem
3.1
also extends to that assumption.
Hence, Theorem
3.1
provides qualitative reasons for which a convergence can be expected under selection. First, the number of selections should be large enough. To quantify this,
let us introduce

(\underline{J}(t))_{t\geq 1}
and
(\bar{J}(t))_{t\geq 1}
two increasing integer
deterministic
sequences and let

\omega(t)=1-\mathbb{P}\big(\underline{J}(t)\leq J(t)\leq\bar{J}(t)\big),\>\>t\geq 1.

(22)

Second, the (random) quantity
D_{t}
(
20
) should be small enough.
This is quantified via a
deterministic
sequence
(\bar{D}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
, tending to zero and nonincreasing in a vicinity of
+\infty
, such that there exists a constant
C_{1}>0
such that

\mathbb{P}(D_{t}>C_{1}\bar{D}_{t})\leq\frac{6\eta_{t}}{\pi^{2}t^{2}},\mbox{ for all $t\geq 1$,}

(23)

for
(\eta_{t})_{t\geq 1}\in(0,1)
some nonincreasing sequence with
\log(1/\eta_{T})\leq\log(T)
. In the non-adaptive case
D_{t}
, we can always choose
\bar{D}_{t}=0
to ensure (
23
).

Our first main result is as follows.

Theorem 3.2
.

[In-probability rate]
Consider the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{T}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{T\geq 1}
with step sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
,
\beta\in(1/2,1)
and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
.
Consider some function
\Pi^{0}
satisfying Assumption
1
with some
q^{0}_{\alpha}\in(0,B)
and the corresponding
D_{t}
being defined by (
20
), which is assumed to be bounded by
(\bar{D}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
as in (
23
), with a rate
(\eta_{t})_{t\geq 1}\in(0,1)
.
Then
(q_{T}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{T\geq 1}
is such that there exist constants
6
6

6

In all the results of the main text, the constants may depend on everything but the time
t
. We refer the reader to the non-asymptotical bounds of §
A
to track the exact dependence of these constants wrt the other quantities.

T_{0}\geq 1
and
C_{0}>0
such that if
T\geq T_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}(|\mathrm{IER}_{T}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{T}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq C_{0}(\rho(T)+\bar{D}_{T}))\geq 1-3\eta_{T}-\omega(t(T))-\omega(T+1),

(24)

where
(\omega(t))_{t\geq 1}
is given by (
22
) and with a sequence
1\ll t(T)<T
, bounds
\underline{J}(T)
,
\bar{J}(T)
and a rate
\rho(T)
given by either of the two following cases:

(i)

\rho(T)=(\log T)^{1/2}T^{1/2-\beta}+T^{\beta-1}
with
\underline{J}(T)\sim cT
,
\bar{J}(T)=T
and
t(T)=cT/2
;

(ii)

\rho(T)=(\log T)^{1/2}\Delta(T)^{1/2}\bar{J}(T)^{-\beta}+\Delta(T)^{-1}\bar{J}(T)^{\beta}
with
\Delta(T):=\underline{J}(T+1)-\bar{J}(t(T))
and any choice of
t(T)
,
\underline{J}(T)
,
\bar{J}(T)
such that

\underline{J}(T+1)\sim\underline{J}(t(T))\sim\bar{J}(T+1)\sim\bar{J}(t(T))
,
\Delta(T)\sim\bar{J}(T+1)-\underline{J}(t(T))
,
\Delta(T)\to\infty
and
\bar{J}(T)^{\beta}\ll\Delta(T)\ll\bar{J}(T)
.

Theorem
3.2
is proved in §
B.6
.
There are two ways to use Theorem
3.2
, either with (i) or (ii). Both are valid, but (ii) leads to a better rate than case (i) under concentration of
J(t)
, while (i) applies to a wider range of situations (see Remark
3.1
below). Markedly, in both cases (i) and (ii), the adaptation can deteriorate the rate only if
\bar{D}_{T}
dominates
\rho(T)
.

Remark 3.1

(Typical rate under concentration)
.

Under concentration property of
J(t)
when
t
grows, a typical choice for the bounds
\underline{J}(t)
and
\bar{J}(t)
are given by
\lfloor rt\pm C(\log t)^{\lambda^{\prime}}t^{\lambda}\rfloor
for some selection rate
r\in(0,1)
and concentration rate exponent
\lambda\in[1/2,1)
(with a constant
C>0
and
\lambda^{\prime}>0
). Then Theorem
3.2
(case (ii)) with
t(T)=T-1\vee\lfloor T^{4\beta/3}\rfloor
leads to
\rho(T)=(\log T)^{1/2}T^{-\beta/3}
when
\beta\in(1/2,3/4)
, with
\beta>3\lambda/4
. It is clear that this rate is better than the one produced by Theorem
3.2
in the case (i) on the range
3\lambda/4<\beta<3/4
. Theorem
3.2
then leads to the optimized rate
\rho(T)+\bar{D}_{T}
with

\displaystyle\rho(T)

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}(\log T)^{1/2}T^{-\beta/3}&\mbox{ if $\beta\in(\beta_{0},3/4)$;}\\
(\log T)^{1/2}T^{1/2-\beta}+T^{\beta-1}&\mbox{ if $\beta\in(1/2,\beta_{0}]\cup[3/4,1)$,}\\
\end{array}}}\right.

with
\beta_{0}:=(1/2)\vee(3\lambda/4)
(hence
\beta_{0}=1/2
and
(1/2,\beta_{0}]=\emptyset
if the concentration holds with
\lambda\leq 2/3
).

Remark 3.2

(Choosing
\beta
)
.

Our in-probability rate provides guidelines for choosing
\beta
in the typical case of Remark
3.1
:

-

simple choice: minimizing the rate
\rho(T)
in (
3.1
) wrt
\beta
suggests to choose
\beta=3/4
, to obtain
\rho(T)=(\log T)^{1/2}T^{-1/4}
with this choice. This is our default recommendation in practice.

-

adaptive choice: recall the smaller
\beta
, the better the bound (
16
) on
\mathrm{FCP}-\alpha
(for the same selection number). Hence, if the order of
\bar{D}_{T}
is known, a choice is to take
\beta\in(1/2,3/4]
the smallest such that
\rho(T)\lesssim\bar{D}_{T}
.

Note that both choices coincide if
\bar{D}_{T}\ll(\log T)^{1/2}T^{-1/4}
. In this case, we obtain the same rate
T^{-1/4}
(up to a log term) both for the
\mathrm{FCP}-\alpha
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathrm{IER}-\alpha}}\right\rvert
bounds.

Remark 3.3

(Restriction to an event)
.

Theorem
3.2
can also be applied when
\omega(t)
in (
22
) and
\bar{D}_{t}
in (
23
) are computed in restriction to the respective events
\Omega_{t}\in\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
, for
t\geq 1
. Assuming that
\mathbb{P}(\Omega)>0
for
\Omega=\cap_{t\geq T_{0}}\Omega_{t}
, the conclusion is that for
t\geq T_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}(|\mathrm{IER}_{T}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{T}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq C_{0}\rho(T)\mid\Omega_{T})\geq 1-C_{0}(\eta_{T}-\omega(t(T))-\omega(T+1)),

(27)

where
C_{0}>0
depends in addition of the value of
\mathbb{P}(\Omega)>0
.

Our second main result provides an in-expectation rate.

Theorem 3.3
.

[In-expectation rate]
Consider the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}

with step sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
,
\beta\in(0,1)
and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
. Assume that for all
t\geq 1
, the selection indicator
S_{t}(X_{t},q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t})
at time
t
is independent of the past
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
.
Consider some function
\Pi^{0}
satisfying Assumption
1
with some
q^{0}_{\alpha}\in(0,B)
and the corresponding
D_{t}
being defined by (
20
) and consider
(\underline{J}(t))_{t\geq 1}
and
(\bar{J}(t))_{t\geq 1}
two sequences tending to infinity, and
(\omega(t))_{t\geq 1}
as in (
22
).
Then, there exists
C_{0}>0
and
T_{0}\geq 1
such that for any
t\geq T_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha}}\right\rvert{\mathbf{1}\{q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}\in[0,B]\}}}}\right]^{2}+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]\leq\>

\displaystyle C_{0}\omega(t)+C_{0}(\underline{J}(t))^{-\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\widetilde{D}_{k}}}\right),

(28)

where
(\widetilde{D}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
is a (deterministic) sequence tending to zero and decreasing on a vicinity of
+\infty
such that
\mathbb{E}[D_{t}]\leq\widetilde{D}_{t}
for all
t\geq 1
.

Theorem
3.3
is proved in §
B.7
. In case of no selective inference (
S_{t}(X,q)\equiv 1
) and no adaptation (
D_{t}=0
) the bounds is of order
t^{-\beta}
and we recover Theorem 2.2 of
Costa and Gadat, 2020
and Proposition 2 of
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
. It thus extends these works to the case of selection and adaptive scores. Note that, by contrast with the in-probability result, the term
1+\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}{\widetilde{D}_{k}}
introduced by adaptation is multiplicative and not additive to the rate
(\underline{J}(t))^{-\beta}
, typically of order
t^{-\beta}
. Hence, the adaptation stage can deteriorate the rate even if
\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}{\widetilde{D}_{k}}
is tending slowly to infinity. This imposes in general to take
\beta
large enough to compensate the adaptation error.

To sum up, Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
showed that, provided that bounding sequences

\underline{J}(t)
,
\bar{J}(t)
can be built for
J(t)
, and provided that
D_{t}
can be bounded, we can obtain explicit convergence rates of the instantaneous error rate to
\alpha
and of the threshold sequence to some
q^{0}_{\alpha}
.
In the next two sections, we apply these results in more concrete examples.

4
Results for
X
-oriented selections

We consider here several selection rules
(S_{t})_{t\geq 1}
, with increasing order of generality:

•

No selective inference:
S_{t}(x,q)\equiv 1
(in which case we let
r:=1
);

•

Time-constant
X
-oriented selection:
S_{t}(x,q)=S(x)
does not depend on
q
and
t
, in which case we assume
r:=\mathbb{P}(S(X_{1})=1)\in(0,1)
;

•

Adaptive
X
-oriented selection:
S_{t}(x,q)=S_{t}(x)
does not depend on
q
, but depends on
t
and
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
.

All these selection rules are referred to as fixed
X
-oriented selections, in the sense that the selection rule does not depend on the threshold decision
q
.
The first case corresponds to the case considered in
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
.
The second case corresponds to the selection of
Bao et al., 2024b
.

Also note that while the regression model is the prominent example of this section, the results of §
4.1
also apply in the classification case.

4.1
Rates for time-constant selection

Corollary 4.1
.

Consider the iid model and a time-constant
X
-oriented selection with selection probability
r\in(0,1]
.
Then the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
with this selection rule, a step sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
,
\beta\in(1/2,1)
and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
is such that there exist
q^{0}_{\alpha}\in(0,B)
,
T_{0}\geq 1

and
C_{0}>0
such that for
t\geq T_{0}
, we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq C_{0}(\rho(t)+\bar{D}_{t})}}\right)\geq 1-7/t,

(29)

where
\rho(t)
is given by (
3.1
) with
\beta_{0}=1/2
,
(\bar{D}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
satisfies (
23
) for
\eta_{t}=1/t
and a sequence
(D_{t})_{t\geq 1}
in (
20
) based on a function
\Pi^{0}
which is arbitrary but must satisfy Assumption
1
with the above
q^{0}_{\alpha}
.

Corollary
4.1
is proved in §
B.8
.
In addition to the previous result, we also have the following in-expectation convergence result.

Corollary 4.2
.

Consider the iid model and a time-constant
X
-oriented selection with selection probability
r\in(0,1]
.
Then the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
with this selection rule, a step sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
,
\beta\in(0,1)
and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
is such that there exist
q^{0}_{\alpha}\in(0,B)
,
T_{0}\geq 1

and
C_{0}>0
such that for
t\geq T_{0}
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha}}\right\rvert{\mathbf{1}\{q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}\in[0,B]\}}}}\right]}}\right)^{2}+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]\leq C_{0}t^{-\beta}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1+\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}\widetilde{D}_{k}}}\right),

(30)

where
(\widetilde{D}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
is a (deterministic) sequence tending to zero and decreasing on a vicinity of
+\infty
such that
\mathbb{E}[D_{t}]\leq\widetilde{D}_{t}
for all
t\geq 1
.
The sequence
(D_{t})_{t\geq 1}
in (
20
) is based on a function
\Pi^{0}
which is arbitrary but must satisfy Assumption
1
with the above
q^{0}_{\alpha}
.

The proof of Corollary
4.2
is provided in §
B.9
. Corollaries
4.1
and
4.2
will be used in the next two sections.

4.2
Rate for iid regression and time-constant selection

Let us consider the iid online regression model

Y_{t}=\mu(X_{t})+\sigma(X_{t})\xi_{t},\>\>t\geq 1,

(31)

for iid couples
(X_{t},\xi_{t})
,
t\geq 1
, following an unknown distribution, and distributed as
(X,\xi)
, where the noise
\xi
satisfies the following assumption:

Assumption 2
.

In the regression model (
31
), the distribution of the noise
\xi
has a symmetric continuous density
f
wrt the Lebesgue measure which is positive on its support with
M_{1}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}f(x)<\infty
,
M_{2}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}\{|x|f(x)\}<\infty
,
M_{3}:=\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}(1/\sigma(x))>0
and
\mathbb{E}\xi^{2}=1
.

Note that this assumption implies in particular
\mathbb{E}[Y_{t}\>|\>X_{t}=x]=\mu(x)
and
\mathbb{V}[Y_{t}\>|\>X_{t}=x]=\sigma^{2}(x)
.
At time
t
, we consider estimators
\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)
and
\hat{\sigma}_{t}(x)
, based on the past
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
, to estimate
\mu(x)
and
\sigma(x)
, respectively. The latter are used to compute the usual residual-based online adaptive score
V_{t}(x,y)
(
6
) with the decision interval
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x,q)
(
7
). This corresponds to the IER function (
19
), benchmark function
\Pi^{0}
and benchmark threshold
q^{0}_{\alpha}
given by

\displaystyle\Pi^{0}(q)

\displaystyle=2\bar{F}(\bar{\Phi}^{-1}((1-q)/2));\quad q^{0}_{\alpha}=1-2\bar{\Phi}(\overline{F}^{-1}(\alpha/2)),

(32)

respectively,
where
\bar{F}
denotes the upper tail of the noise
\xi
distribution.

Proposition 4.3
.

For the online iid regression model (
31
) with Assumption
2
, consider the distance
D_{t}\leq\|\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}-\Pi^{0}\|_{\infty}
(
20
) for any (not necessarily time-constant)

X
-oriented selection rule
S_{t}(\cdot)
and the IER function (
18
) and benchmark function
\Pi^{0}
given by (
19
) and (
32
), respectively.
Then, for all
t\geq 1
, we have almost surely

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}-\Pi^{0}\|_{\infty}\leq M_{3}\>\mathbb{E}[M_{1}|\hat{\mu}_{t}(X_{t})-{\mu}(X_{t})|+(M_{2}\vee 1)|\hat{\sigma}_{t}(X_{t})-{\sigma}(X_{t})|\>|\>S_{t}(X_{t})=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}].

(33)

In particular, for all
t\geq 1
, we have almost surely

\displaystyle\|\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}-\Pi^{0}\|_{\infty}\leq\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S_{t}(X_{t})=1}}\right)^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(M_{3}\>\mathbb{E}[M_{1}|\hat{\mu}_{t}(X_{t})-{\mu}(X_{t})|+(M_{2}\vee 1)|\hat{\sigma}_{t}(X_{t})-{\sigma}(X_{t})|\>|\>\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]}}\right).

(34)

Proposition
4.3
is proved in §
B.10
. This shows that
D_{t}
converges appropriately to zero when choosing appropriate estimators
\hat{\mu}_{t}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{t}
.
More precisely, we consider the following convergence properties: for
t
large enough and some constant
C_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}[\mathbb{E}[|\hat{\mu}_{t}(X_{t})-{\mu}(X_{t})|+|\hat{\sigma}_{t}(X_{t})-{\sigma}(X_{t})|\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\geq C_{1}(\log t)^{\nu_{0}}t^{-\nu}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{3}{\pi^{2}t^{3}};

(35)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\mu}_{t}(X_{t})-{\mu}(X_{t}))^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\sigma}_{t}(X_{t})-{\sigma}(X_{t}))^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(\log t)^{2\tau_{0}}t^{-2\tau},

(36)

for some
\nu_{0},\tau_{0}>0
and
\nu,\tau\in(0,1/2]
.
As a typical instance, if
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]^{d}
, and
\mu(\cdot)
and
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)
are Lipschitz functions, there exists kernel-based estimators
\hat{\mu}_{t}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{t}
(based upon the
t-1
sample
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
) such that (
35
) and (
36
) holds with
\nu=\tau=1/(2+d)

(
Tsybakov, 2009
;
Wang et al., 2008
)
. However, any function classe can be chosen for
\mu(\cdot)
and
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)
, with a corresponding estimator class and convergence rate, as for instance those based upon Neural Network
(
Schmidt-Hieber, 2020
)
.

For a time-constant
X
-oriented selection rule, the bound (
34
) leads to condition (
23
) with
\bar{D}_{t}=O((\log t)^{\nu_{0}}t^{-\nu})
and
\widetilde{D}_{t}=O((\log t)^{\tau_{0}}t^{-\tau})
.
To combine further this rate with Corollary
4.1
and Corollary
4.2
and obtain convergence rates, we check that Assumption
1
is satisfied with
B=1
and
\Pi^{0}
,
q^{0}_{\alpha}
given in (
32
).
Clearly,
\Pi^{0}
is decreasing and

(\Pi^{0})^{\prime}(q)=-\frac{f}{\phi}\big(\Phi^{-1}\big(\frac{1-q}{2}\big)\big),\>q\in(0,1),

is a continuous function on the compact interval
[q^{0}_{t,\alpha}/2,(1+q^{0}_{t,\alpha})/2]
. In addition,
(\Pi^{0})^{\prime}(q^{0}_{\alpha})=-\frac{f}{\phi}\big(\overline{F}^{-1}\big(\alpha/2\big)\big)>0
.
This leads to the following result.

Corollary 4.4
.

Consider the online iid regression model (
31
) satisfying Assumption
2
and consider
q^{0}_{\alpha}
given by (
32
).
Then the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
using a time-constant
X
-oriented selection with
r=\mathbb{P}(S(X)=1)\in(0,1]
, a prediction interval

\mathcal{C}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(x)=\big[\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)\pm\hat{\sigma}_{t}(x)\Phi^{-1}\big(\frac{q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}+1}{2}\big)\big]

with
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable estimators
\hat{\mu}_{t},\hat{\sigma}_{t}
, a step sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
,
\beta\in(0,1)
, and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
is such that there exist

T_{0}\geq 1
and
C_{0}>0
such that for
t\geq T_{0}
,

•

In-probability rate: choosing any
\beta\in(1/2,1)
and assuming that the estimators satisfy (
35
), we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha|+|q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}|\leq C_{0}(\rho(t)+(\log t)^{\nu_{0}}t^{-\nu})}}\right)\geq 1-1/t.

(37)

for
\rho(t)
given by (
3.1
) with
\beta_{0}=1/2
.

•

In-expectation rate: choosing
\beta>1-\tau
and assuming that the estimators satisfy (
36
), we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha}}\right\rvert{\mathbf{1}\{q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}\in[0,B]\}}}}\right]}}\right)^{2}+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}}}\right]\leq C_{0}(\log t)^{\tau_{0}}t^{1-\tau-\beta}.

(38)

Note that (
37
) shows that
\mathbb{P}(q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}\notin[0,B])\leq 1/t
. This can in turn be used into (
38
) to remove the indicator
{\mathbf{1}\{q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}\in[0,B]\}}
in the IER expectation (by assuming both (
35
) and (
36
)).

According to Remark
3.2
, if
\nu
is known, an adaptive choice of
\beta
in function of
\nu
can be done in (
37
), by letting
\beta=(3\nu)\wedge(3/4)\vee(1/2+\epsilon)
, for
\epsilon
arbitrary small. For instance, when
\nu=1/(2+d)
for a dimension
d\geq 1
, this gives
\beta=3/4-\epsilon
for
d\in\{1,2\}
,
\beta=3/5
for
d=3
and
\beta=1/2+\epsilon
for
d\geq 4
.

4.3
Conditional coverage and optimality

Corollary
4.4
shows that, in online iid regression model (
31
) and time constant selection, the threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
of the
OnlineSCI
procedure does converge to
q^{0}_{\alpha}
(
32
) at some rate both in expectation and with high probability.
In section, we show that, under mild assumptions, the two following additional properties hold:

•

for iid online regression, the
OnlineSCI
procedure based on the adaptive score produces prediction sets with asymptotical conditional coverage, that is, conditionally on
X=x
for any
x
;

•

any other method satisfying this asymptotical conditional coverage should produce prediction sets with a larger averaged length, up to some vanishing terms.

In a nutshell, the first point comes from the fact that
q^{0}_{\alpha}
in (
32
) does not depend on the selection. The second point comes from the following assumption (made in addition to Assumption
2
):

Assumption 3
.

In the regression model (
31
), the density
f
of
\xi
is decreasing on
[0,+\infty)
. Also,
\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\sigma(x)<+\infty
.

With Assumption
3
,
the prediction set of
OnlineSCI
approaches

{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(x)=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mu(x)\pm\sigma(x)\overline{F}^{-1}(\alpha/2)}}\right]=\{y\in\mathcal{Y}\>:\>f_{Y|X=x}(y)\geq c^{*}(x)\},

(39)

with
f_{Y|X=x}(x)=f(|y-\mu(x)|/\sigma(x))/\sigma(x)
being the density of the distribution of
Y
given
X=x
and
c^{*}(x)=f(\overline{F}^{-1}(\alpha/2))/\sigma(x)
. Hence,
{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(x)
is a level set of the conditional density that satisfies the conditional coverage
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y\in{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(x)\mid X=x}}\right)=1-\alpha
, and it is well known that its length
\sigma(x)\overline{F}^{-1}(\alpha/2)
is minimal among all prediction sets with conditional coverage at least
1-\alpha
, see
Scott and Nowak, 2006
among others.
Combining this with Corollary
4.4
shows the optimality result.

Formally, consider the slightly stronger pointwise version of convergence condition (
36
) for
\hat{\mu}_{t}
and
\hat{\sigma}_{t}
: for
t
large enough and some constant
C_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)-{\mu}(x))^{2}]+\sup_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mathbb{E}[(\hat{\sigma}_{t}(x)-{\sigma}(x))^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq C_{1}(\log t)^{2\tau_{0}}t^{-2\tau},

(40)

for some
\tau_{0}>0
and
\tau\in(0,1/2]
.

Corollary 4.5
.

Consider the online iid regression model (
31
) satisfying Assumptions
2
and
3
.
Consider the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
using a time-constant
X
-oriented selection with
r=\mathbb{P}(S(X)=1)\in(0,1]
, a prediction interval (
7
) with
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable estimators
\hat{\mu}_{t},\hat{\sigma}_{t}
satisfying (
35
)-(
40
), a step sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
,
\beta\in(1/2,1)
, and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
.
Then this procedure is such that:

(i)

it has approximate conditional coverage: the produced sequence
\mathcal{C}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(x)=\Big[\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)\pm\hat{\sigma}_{t}(x)\Phi^{-1}\Big(\frac{q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}+1}{2}\Big)\Big]
,
t\geq 1,x\in\mathcal{X}
, of prediction sets is such that

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})\mid X_{t}=x}}\right)\geq 1-\alpha-C_{0}[\rho(t)+(\log t)^{\tau_{0}}t^{-\tau}+(\log t)^{\nu_{0}}t^{-\nu}],\mbox{ for all }x\in\mathcal{X},

(41)

for some constant
C_{0}>0
and
\rho(t)
as in (
3.1
);

(ii)

it mimics the oracle procedure: choosing
\beta>1-\tau
,
for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and
M>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|{\mathcal{C}}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(x)|\wedge M}}\right]\leq|{\mathcal{C}}^{*}(x)|+C_{0}(\log t)^{\tau_{0}/2}t^{1/2-\tau/2-\beta/2}+M/t,

(42)

for some constant
C_{0}>0
(depending on all other constants but not on
M
).

(iii)

it approximately dominates any other method with approximate conditional coverage: choosing
\beta>1-\tau
,
for any method producing a sequence
{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{t}(x),t\geq 1,x\in\mathcal{X}
of (
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable) prediction set rules with the conditional coverage property
\forall x\in\mathcal{X}
,
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{t}\in{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{t}(x)\mid X_{t}=x}}\right)\geq 1-\alpha-\eta^{\prime}_{t}
, for some rate
\eta^{\prime}_{t}>0
tending to zero, we have for
t
large enough, for all
x\in\mathcal{X}
, and
M>0
,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|{\mathcal{C}}_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}(x)|\wedge M}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{t}(x)|}}\right]+C_{0}(\log t)^{\tau_{0}/2}t^{1/2-\tau/2-\beta/2}+C_{0}\eta^{\prime}_{t}+M/t,

(43)

for some constant
C_{0}>0
(depending on all other constants but not on
M
).

Corollary
4.5
is proved in §
B.11
. It relies both on the in-probability and in-expectation bounds obtained in Corollary
4.4
. A byproduct of (
43
) is that, choosing
\beta>1-\tau
, and
M=\sqrt{t}
, we have

\limsup_{t\to\infty}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|{\mathcal{C}}_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}(x)|\wedge\sqrt{t}}}\right]-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|{\mathcal{C}}^{\prime}_{t}(x)|}}\right]}}\right)\leq 0.

Hence, Corollary
4.5
states that no method can outperform
OnlineSCI
by a large amount, which is an optimality property. While other methods could also satisfy a similar optimality property (as the oracle one if
q^{0}_{\alpha}
is known), recall that
OnlineSCI
also markedly has a valid FCP bound (
16
) in the adversarial model. Hence, it makes the best of two worlds.

4.4
Rates for iid regression and adaptive selection

Sections
4.1
and
4.2
considered the case of a time-constant selection. However, it can be of interest to consider a selection that varies in time. This section deals with the latter, in the iid regression model (
31
).

We focus on an example, inspired from
Bao et al., 2024b
(in the batch setting), where we seek for large outcome: one aims at selecting
X_{t}
’s likely to correspond to a large outcome
Y_{t}
. Ideally, we would like to choose
S_{t}(x)=S^{*}(x)={\mathbf{1}\{\mu(x)\geq y_{0}\}}
for some given value
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
, which is a time-constant selection that could be handled by the previous section. However,
\mu(\cdot)
is unknown by essence and this selection rule cannot be applied. As a surrogate, one can opt for the adaptive
X
-oriented selection:

S_{t}(x)={\mathbf{1}\{\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)\geq y_{0}\}},

(44)

where
\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)
is a suitable estimator of the conditional mean
\mu(x)
of
Y
given
X=x
. Considering the decision interval
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x,q)
(
7
) based on an online adaptive score
V_{t}(x,y)
(
6
), the IER function (
18
)
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}(q)
(
19
) gets close to

\Pi^{0}
in (
32
) with a distance
D_{t}
bounded via Proposition
4.3
. Since these functions are the same as in the previous section, and Assumptions
7
and
8
are satisfied. However, we cannot apply here Corollary
4.1
to obtain rate because the selection (
44
) is not time-constant. Also, using our in-expectation results (Theorem
3.3
) is not possible here because
S_{t}(X_{t})
is not independent of
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
.
Nevertheless, our in-probability result (Theorem
3.2
) can be applied: it remains to find high probability bounds for the selection number
J(t)
(
13
), which is doable since
\mathbb{P}(\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)\geq y_{0})
gets close to
r=\mathbb{P}(\mu(X)\geq y_{0})
when
t
gets large, under mild assumptions. This reasoning leads to the following result.

Assumption 4
.

In the regression model (
31
),
\mu(X)
has a density in a neighborhood of
y_{0}
which is bounded by
M_{4}>0
.

Corollary 4.6
.

Consider the online iid regression model (
31
) satisfying Assumptions
2
and
4
.
Then the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
with a prediction interval (
7
) using
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable estimators
\hat{\mu}_{t},\hat{\sigma}_{t}
satisfying (
35
),
the
X
-oriented selection (
44
), a step sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
,
with
\beta\in(1/2,1)

and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
is such that there exist

T_{0}\geq 1
and
C_{0}>0
such that for
t\geq T_{0}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha|+|q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}|\leq C_{0}(\rho(t)+(\log t)^{\nu_{0}}t^{-\nu})}}\right)\geq 1-1/t,

(45)

with
\rho(t)
given by (
3.1
) for
\beta_{0}=(3/4)(1-\nu/2)\in(1/2,3/4)
.

Corollary
4.6
is proved in §
B.12
.
In a nutshell, the difference with the time-constant selection of §
4.2
is that the estimation error slows down the concentration of
J(t)
here, which impacts the convergence rate when
\beta\leq\beta_{0}
.

4.5
Rate for auto-regressive model with no selective inference

Theorems
3.2
and
3.3
are valid beyond the iid model, and we illustrate in this section how they can be applied in a specific dependent context, by considering an autoregressive time series of order
d\geq 1
, for which no selective inference is made (hence
OnlineSCI
reduces here essentially to the method of
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
).

Let us consider a centered
\mbox{AR}(d)
process with Gaussian innovations, parameterized by
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
:

Y_{t}=\sum_{k=1}^{d}\varphi_{k}Y_{t-k}+Z_{t},\ \forall t\in\mathbb{Z},

(46)

where the variables
(Z_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z})}
are iid
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
(with
\sigma^{2}=1
), and
(Y_{t})_{t\in\mathbb{Z}}
is a stationary process. We assume for simplicity that
\mathbb{E}(Y_{t})=0
for all
t\in\mathbb{Z}
and we assume that the polynomial
P(z)=1-\sum_{k=1}^{d}\varphi_{k}z^{k}
has no root of modulus smaller or equal to
1
, which ensures that the model (
46
) is well defined
(
Anderson, 2011
)
.

We consider the setting where we predict
Y_{t}
from the covariate
X_{t}=(Y_{t-1},\dots,Y_{t-d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which has the specific property to be
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable, because
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}=\sigma(Y_{-(n-1)},\dots,Y_{t-1})
here. In this setting, the holdout set
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
is not independent from
(X_{t},Y_{t})_{t\geq 1}
and we assume that the whole observed process
(X_{t},Y_{t})_{-(n-1)\leq t}
is as in (
46
). For adaptation, we consider an
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable estimator
\widehat{\varphi}_{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
of
\varphi
and the corresponding estimator
\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)=\langle\widehat{\varphi}_{t};x\rangle
of
\mu(x)=\langle\varphi;x\rangle
, where
\langle\cdot;\cdot\rangle
denotes the inner product in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. With this estimator and
F=\Phi
(and
\hat{\sigma}_{t}(x)=1
), we consider the adaptive score
V_{t}(x,y)
(
6
), the prediction interval
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x,q)
(
7
), which leads to a benchmark function
\Pi^{0}
and benchmark threshold
q^{0}_{\alpha}
, as in (
32
) (note that
q^{0}_{\alpha}=1-\alpha
here).
In addition, there exists
\widehat{\varphi}_{t}
such that (
35
) and (
36
) are satisfied in the
\mbox{AR}(d)
model with
\nu_{0}=3/2
,
\nu=1/2
,
\tau_{0}=0
and
\tau=1/2
. For instance, this is the case by using the regularized least square method
(
Goldenshluger and Zeevi, 2001
)
.
We thus obtain a result similar to Corollary
4.4
in the (non iid) auto-regressive case.

Corollary 4.7
.

Consider the online stationary
\mbox{AR}(d)
model (
46
) with Gaussian innovations, parameterized by
\varphi\in\mathbb{R}^{p}
.
Then the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
with no selective inference (i.e.
S_{t}(x,q)\equiv 1
), a prediction interval

\mathcal{C}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(x)=\big[\langle\widehat{\varphi}_{t};x\rangle\pm\Phi^{-1}\big(\frac{q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}+1}{2}\big)\big]

with a
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable estimator
\widehat{\phi}_{t}
, a step sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
,
\beta\in(0,1)
, and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
is such that there exist

T_{0}\geq 1
and
C_{0}>0
such that for
t\geq T_{0}
,

•

In-probability rate: choosing any
\beta\in(1/2,1)
