and assuming that the estimator
\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)=\langle\widehat{\varphi}_{t};x\rangle
satisfies
(
35
) with
\nu_{0}=3/2
,
\nu=1/2
, we have

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha|+|q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-(1-\alpha)|\leq C_{0}(\rho(t)+(\log t)^{3/2}t^{-1/2})}}\right)\geq 1-1/t.

(47)

for
\rho(t)
given by (
3.1
) with
\beta_{0}=1/2
.

•

In-expectation rate: choosing
\beta>1/2
and assuming that the estimator
\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)=\langle\widehat{\varphi}_{t};x\rangle
satisfies (
36
) with
\tau_{0}=0
,
\tau=1/2
, we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha}}\right\rvert{\mathbf{1}\{q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}\in[0,B]\}}}}\right]}}\right)^{2}+\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-(1-\alpha))}}\right)^{2}}}\right]\leq C_{0}t^{-\beta+1/2}.

(48)

Corollary
4.7
is proved in §
B.13
. It is to our knowledge the first result that shows a convergence for the threshold/IER of an ACI-type method in the auto-regressive model. This model was also considered in
Zaffran et al., 2022
(Example A.3) with
d=1
, but the problem and criterion therein was markedly different (convergence of the average length of a partially oracle (e.g., no adaptation) ACI prediction set).

5
Results for informative selections

This section presents results for selection that are ‘informative’ or ’decision-oriented’, in the sense that the selection
S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}})
does dependent on the actual threshold
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}
at time
t
. Throughout the section, we consider the iid model. Also, since
S_{t}(X,q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}})
is by essence not independent of
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
for informative selection, the in-expectation result (Theorem
3.3
) cannot be used, and we will rely on the in-probability bound (Theorem
3.2
) throughout this section.

5.1
Incompressible failure probability and criticality

Considering a selection depending on the actual
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}
changes formally the behavior of
OnlineSCI
. We focus in this section to selection rules
S(x,q)
of the form (
5
) for some (possibly adaptive) test statistics
W_{t}(x)
.
Recall that Algorithm
OnlineSCI
(without the restarting adjustment of Section
2.3
) does not select anymore after time
t
as soon as
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}\geq B
, hence,
q_{T}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}=q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}
for
T\geq t
. The probability that
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}\geq B
for some
t
is typically positive (although small), see Lemma
C.2
for an example. This means that we cannot expect that
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}
converges with an arbitrary small probability in the informative case.

To quantify theoretically the performance of the algorithm
OnlineSCI
in the informative case we will thus make appear an incompressible ’probability of being unlucky’ term, that we will denote by
\delta
, and establish convergence results holding with probability at least
1-\delta
(approximately). More formally, we will assume in this section that, when
t
is large, the test statistic
W_{t}(x)
gets close to
W(x)
, a random variable satisfying the following assumption:

Assumption 5
.

W(X)
has a continuous density supported in
[\kappa,B^{*}]
, which is positive on
(\kappa,B^{*})
, for some
0\leq\kappa<B^{*}\leq B
.

Formally, the incompressible probability of failure of
OnlineSCI
is defined by

\delta:=\inf_{q\in[0,B^{*})}\inf_{t\geq 1}\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sup_{T\geq t}q_{T}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}>q}}\right)\in[0,1],

(49)

Note that the probability
\delta
is a bit greater than the probability of
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}
being frozen above
B
(as evoked above). First, it includes the case where
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}
exceeds
B^{*}
(upper support bound defined in (
49
)), which could be strictly less than
B
. This is to accommodate the limiting test statistics
W(x)
which can only reach
B^{*}\leq B
(while
W_{t}(x)
may reach
B
for some
t
). Second,
\delta
includes also the case where
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}
can arbitrary approach
B^{*}
by below and infinitely often. Nevertheless,
\delta
is incompressible: Lemma
C.3
states that it is impossible to provide that
\mathbb{P}(\lim_{t}q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}=q_{\alpha}^{0})>1-\delta
. On the other hand, by definition (
49
) and the basic property of an infimum, we have that for all
\delta^{\prime}>\delta
there exists
T_{0}=T_{0}(\delta^{\prime})
and
\bar{q}=\bar{q}(\delta^{\prime})\in(0,B^{*})
such that
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sup_{T\geq T_{0}}q_{T}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}\leq\bar{q}}}\right)>1-\delta^{\prime}
, hence,
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}
is uniformly away from
B^{*}
by below with probability
1-\delta^{\prime}
. Under mild conditions, we show in the next sections that this is enough to state that
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}
converges well with probability greater than
1-\delta^{\prime}
minus a vanishing term.

Next, in the three examples of this section, it turns out that the benchmark function
\Pi^{0}
can be written as (with
B=1
):

\displaystyle\Pi^{0}(q)

\displaystyle=1-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[W(X)\mid W(X)>q}}\right],\>\>\>q\in[0,1],

(50)

with the convention
\Pi^{0}(q)=1-B^{*}
when
\mathbb{P}(W(X)>q)=0
.
By Lemma
C.7
, under Assumption
5
,
\Pi^{0}
is decreasing on
[\kappa,B^{*}]
and continuous on
[0,1]
with
\Pi^{0}(0)=\Pi^{0}(\kappa)=1-\mathbb{E}[W(X)]
and
\Pi^{0}(B^{*})=\Pi^{0}(1)=1-B^{*}
. In addition,

\Pi^{0}
is differentiable on
(\kappa,B^{*})
, with a continuous derivative on any compact set of
(\kappa,B^{*})
which is away from
0
.
As a result, if
\alpha\in(1-B^{*},1-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[W(X)}}\right])
, the benchmark function
\Pi^{0}
satisfies Assumption
1
with
q_{\alpha}^{0}\in(\kappa,1-\alpha)
such that

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[W(X)\mid W(X)>q_{\alpha}^{0}}}\right]=1-\alpha.

(51)

Note that the condition
\alpha<1-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[W(X)}}\right]
is not restrictive; it excludes the too favorable situation where
W(X)\approx 1
for which it is not possible to reach an
\alpha
level error. By contrast, the condition
\alpha>1-B^{*}
is a limitation when
B^{*}<1
, that is, when the variable
W(X)
cannot be arbitrarily close to
1
: our results cannot accommodate error level smaller than

\alpha^{*}:=1-B^{*}.

(52)

This phenomenon is not new in batch multiple testing, and is referred to as criticality
(
Chi, 2007
)
. We show here that it can also occur in the online setting when
B^{*}<1
(that is,
\alpha^{*}>0
).

5.2
Rate and optimality for online selective classification

Consider the iid classification model, with features in
[0,1]^{d}
(for simplicity), that is,

(X_{t},Y_{t})\in[0,1]^{d}\times[K],t\geq 1,\mbox{ iid }\sim(X,Y),

(53)

with an arbitrary distribution.
Consider the problem of selecting examples for which a point-prediction set (that is an usual classification) is made. For this, consider
the score/test functions

\displaystyle V_{t}(x,y)

\displaystyle:=\hat{}\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\neq y\mid X_{t}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t-1});

(54)

\displaystyle W_{t}(x)

\displaystyle:=\max_{y\in[K]}\bar{V}_{t}(x,y),

(55)

where
\hat{}\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\neq y\mid X_{t}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t-1})
is some estimator of
V(x,y):=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y\neq y\mid X=x}}\right)
. This means that
\bar{V}_{t}(x,y):=1-V_{t}(x,y)=\hat{}\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}=y\mid X_{t}=x,\mathcal{F}_{t-1})
is the corresponding estimator of
\bar{V}(x,y):=1-V(x,y)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y=y\mid X=x}}\right)
.
The particular form of the method
OnlineSCI
in this case uses the prediction set rule (
4
) and the selection rule (
5
), and is summarized in Algorithm
2
.

We consider the following assumption on the estimator sequence
(\bar{V}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
: for a
t
large enough and
C_{1}>0
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\max_{y\in[K]}|\bar{V}_{t}(X_{t},y)-\bar{V}(X_{t},y)|\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}}\right]\geq C_{1}(\log t)^{\nu_{0}}t^{-\nu}}}\right)\leq\frac{3}{\pi^{2}t^{3}},

(56)

for
\nu_{0}>0
and
\nu\in(0,1/2]
. This property is classical by assuming that the functions
x\mapsto\bar{V}(x,y)
,
y\in[K]
, belong to some regular classes, with various estimation techniques
(
Tsybakov and Audibert, 2007
;
Bos and Schmidt-Hieber, 2022
)
.

In addition, the IER function, given by
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}(q)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W_{t}(X_{t},y)>\bar{V}_{t}(X_{t},Y_{t})\mid W_{t}(X_{t})>q,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}}\right)
in (
18
) is intended to get close to the benchmark function
\Pi^{0}(q)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(W(X,y)>\bar{V}(X,Y)\mid W(X)>q}}\right)
when
t
grows, for

W(x):=\max_{y\in[K]}\bar{V}(x,y)\in[1/K,1].

(57)

We easily check that
\Pi^{0}(q)
is of the form (
50
).
Hence, the following result holds.

Theorem 5.1
.

Consider the iid classification model (
53
) satisfying Assumption
5
with
W(x)
given by (
57
) and support values
1/K\leq\kappa\leq B^{*}\leq 1
, and respective estimators
(V_{t}(x,y))_{x\in[0,1]^{d},y\in[K],t\geq 1}
of the class probabilities
(\mathbb{P}(Y=y\mid X=x))_{x\in[0,1]^{d},y\in[K]}
satisfying (
56
).
Let an error level
\alpha\in(0,1)
such that
1-B^{*}<\alpha<1-\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[W(X)}}\right]
. Consider the
OnlineSCI
selective classification method
\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}=(S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t}),\hat{Y}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t}))_{t\geq 1}
with
\hat{Y}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})=\arg\max_{y\in[K]}\bar{V}_{t}(X_{t},y)
and
S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})={\mathbf{1}\{\max_{y\in[K]}\bar{V}_{t}(X_{t},y)>q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\}}
, for the threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
given by Algorithm
2
with step sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
,
\beta\in(1/2,1)
and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,1-\alpha]
.
Consider
\delta
given in (
49
). Then for any
\delta^{\prime}>\delta
, there exist
T_{0}=T_{0}(\delta^{\prime})\geq 1
and
C_{0}=C_{0}(\delta^{\prime})>0
such that for all
t\geq T_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq C_{0}(\rho(t)+(\log t)^{\nu_{0}/2}t^{-\nu/2})}}\right)\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}-C_{0}/t,

(58)

for
\rho(t)=(\log t)^{1/2}t^{1/2-\beta}+t^{\beta-1}
,
q^{0}_{\alpha}
the only point of
(\kappa,1-\alpha)
satisfying (
51
) and where
\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\neq\hat{Y}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})\mid S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1})
.

Theorem
5.1
is proved in §
B.14
. It provides an explicit convergence rate for the IER, which is slightly slower than (
3.1
) for
\beta<3/4
: while optimizing
\rho(t)
wrt
\beta
suggests to choose
\beta=3/4
, we can also opt for
\beta=(1-\nu/2)\wedge(1/2+\nu/2)\leq 3/4
when
\nu
is known, which diminishes
\beta
and provide the same global rate
\rho(t)+(\log t)^{\nu_{0}/2}t^{-\nu/2}\propto t^{-\nu/2}
(up to log terms).

Theorem
5.1
states that the
OnlineSCI
procedure gets close to the procedure selecting at time
t
if
S^{*}(X_{t})=1
and predicting the class
Y^{*}(X_{t})
, where

\displaystyle Y^{*}(x)

\displaystyle=\arg\max_{y\in[K]}\{\bar{V}(x,y)\};

(59)

\displaystyle S^{*}(x)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{1}\{W(x)>q_{\alpha}^{0}\}}.

(60)

Reformulating Theorem 1 of
Zhao and Su, 2023
in our context, the latter procedure is optimal in the sense that it maximizes the probability of correct selection among all selection rules and classifier with IER control at level
\alpha
(see Proposition
B.5
for a, slightly more general, detailed statement).
Combined with Theorem
5.1
, this implies the following result.

Corollary 5.2
.

In the setting of Theorem
5.1
, the corresponding online selective classification method
\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}=(S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t}),\hat{Y}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t}))_{t\geq 1}
is such that: for any
\delta^{\prime}>\delta
with
\delta^{\prime}<1-\alpha
, there exists
C_{0}=C_{0}(\delta^{\prime})
and
T_{0}=T_{0}(\delta^{\prime})
, such that we have for
\rho(t)=(\log t)^{1/2}(t^{1/2-\beta}+t^{1-\beta})
,

(i)

IER control: for all
t\geq T_{0}
,
\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\neq\hat{Y}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})\mid S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})=1)\leq\alpha+\delta^{\prime}+C_{0}(\rho(t)+(\log t)^{\nu_{0}/2}t^{-\nu/2})
.

(ii)

Mimicking the oracle: for all
t\geq T_{0}
,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}={Y}^{*}(X_{t})\}}S^{*}(X_{t})}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}=\hat{Y}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})\}}S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})\big]+C_{0}(\rho(t)+(\log t)^{\nu_{0}/2}t^{-\nu/2})+\delta^{\prime}.

(iii)

Approximately dominating all other IER controlling procedures:
for any selective classification procedure
(S_{t}(X_{t}),\hat{Y}_{t}(X_{t}))_{t\geq 1}
(for which each function
S_{t}(\cdot)
and
\hat{Y}_{t}(\cdot)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable) is dominated by
OnlineSCI
in the following sense: if for all
t\geq T_{0}
,
\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\neq\hat{Y}_{t}(X_{t})\mid S_{t}(X_{t})=1)\leq\alpha+\delta^{\prime}+\eta_{t}
, for some rate
\eta_{t}\to 0
, then for all
t\geq T_{0}

\mathbb{E}\big[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}=\hat{Y}_{t}(X_{t})\}}S_{t}(X_{t})\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}=\hat{Y}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})\}}S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})\big]+C_{0}(\rho(t)+(\log t)^{\nu_{0}/2}t^{-\nu/2})+C\delta^{\prime}+C\eta_{t},

(61)

where
C>0
is a constant only depending on
\alpha
(and not on
\delta^{\prime}
).

Corollary
5.2
is proved in §
B.15.2
. It says that
OnlineSCI
is optimal up to remainder terms that are small if
\delta
(
49
) is small and
t
is large.

5.3
Rate and optimality for online conformal testing

Consider the iid online testing setting

(X_{t},Y_{t})\in[0,1]^{d}\times\{0,1\},t\geq 1,\mbox{ iid }\sim(X,Y),

(62)

with an arbitrary distribution. Recall that we wish to test “
Y_{t}=0
” versus “
Y_{t}=1
” along time
t
.
Formally, we consider the prediction set
{\mathcal{C}}_{t}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{1}}\right\}
and the selection function (
55
) with the statistics

\displaystyle W_{t}(x)

\displaystyle:=1-\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}(x),

(63)

where the local false discovery rate
(
Efron et al., 2001
)
is defined by:

\displaystyle{\mathrm{lfdr}}(x)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{P}(Y=0\mid X=x),

(64)

and
\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}(x)
is a
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-estimate of
\mathrm{lfdr}(x)
at time
t
.
The particular form of the method
OnlineSCI
in this case is given by Algorithm
1
.

The IER function
\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}(q)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{t}=0\mid 1-\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}(X_{t})>q,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}}\right)
in (
18
) is close to the benchmark function

\Pi^{0}(q)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y=0\mid 1-{\mathrm{lfdr}}(X)>q}}\right),

(65)

which is termed the positive FDR
(
Storey, 2003
;
Efron, 2008
)
in the multiple testing literature, for the (optimal) case that the rejection region is
1-\mathrm{lfdr}(X)>q
.
We easily check that (
65
) is of the form (
50
) for
W(x):=1-{\mathrm{lfdr}}(x)
so that the desired assumptions hold for conformal testing.
We consider the following assumption on the estimator sequence
(\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}(x))_{t\geq 1}
: for
C_{1}>0
and
t
large enough,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[|\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}(X_{t})-\mathrm{lfdr}(X_{t})|\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}}\right]\geq C_{1}(\log t)^{\nu_{0}}t^{-\nu}}}\right)\leq\frac{3}{\pi^{2}t^{3}}.

(66)

This assumption is met under regularity condition for
x\mapsto\mathrm{lfdr}(x)

(
Tsybakov and Audibert, 2007
;
Bos and Schmidt-Hieber, 2022
)
.

Theorem 5.3
.

Consider the iid testing model (
62
) satisfying Assumption
5
with the test statistics
W(x):=1-{\mathrm{lfdr}}(x)
(
64
) and support values
0\leq\kappa<B^{*}\leq 1
, and respective estimators
(\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}(x))_{x\in[0,1]^{d},t\geq 1}
of
(\mathrm{lfdr}(x))_{x\in[0,1]^{d}}
satisfying (
66
).
Let an error level
\alpha\in(0,1)
be such that
1-B^{*}=:\alpha^{*}<\alpha<\mathbb{P}(Y=0)
. Consider the online conformal testing method
\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}=(S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t}),\{1\})_{t\geq 1}
with
S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})={\mathbf{1}\{\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}(X_{t})<1-q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\}}
, for the threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
given by Algorithm
2
with step sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
,
\beta\in(1/2,1)
and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,1-\alpha]
.
Consider
\delta
given in (
49
). Then for any
\delta^{\prime}>\delta
, there exist
T_{0}=T_{0}(\delta^{\prime})\geq 1
and
C_{0}=C_{0}(\delta^{\prime})>0
such that for all
t\geq T_{0}
,

\mathbb{P}(|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha|+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq C_{0}(\rho(t)+(\log t)^{\nu_{0}/2}t^{-\nu/2}))\geq 1-\delta^{\prime}-C_{0}/t,

(67)

for
\rho(t)=(\log t)^{1/2}t^{1/2-\beta}+t^{\beta-1}
,
q^{0}_{\alpha}
the only point of
(\kappa,1-\alpha)
satisfying (
51
) and where
\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})=\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}=0\mid S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1})
is the instantaneous false discovery rate.

Theorem
5.3
is proved in §
B.16
.
Note that Assumption
5
with the test statistics
W(x):=1-{\mathrm{lfdr}}(x)
means that the distribution of
\mathrm{lfdr}(X)
has a continuous density on
[\alpha^{*},1-\kappa]
, which is positive on
(\alpha^{*},1-\kappa)
. In particular, there is no criticality (
\alpha^{*}=0
) if
\mathrm{lfdr}(X)
has a distribution that can be arbitrary close to
0
, which means that null observations and alternative observations can be arbitrarily well separated with positive probability. If
\alpha^{*}>0
, however, they cannot be arbitrarily well separated and Theorem
5.3
imposes
\alpha>\alpha^{*}
to have the desired high probability convergence rate.

Theorem
5.3
ensures that the
OnlineSCI
method is close to the oracle testing procedure

\displaystyle S^{*}(x)

\displaystyle={\mathbf{1}\{\mathrm{lfdr}(x)<1-q_{\alpha}^{0}\}}.

(68)

This procedure corresponds to the oracle procedure of
Sun and Cai, 2007
.
Reformulating Theorem 1 of
Sun and Cai, 2007
in our context, the latter procedure is optimal in the sense that it maximizes the probability of correct detection among all detection rules with IER control at level
\alpha
(see Proposition
B.6
for a slightly more general and detailed statement).

Corollary 5.4
.

In the setting of Theorem
5.3
, the corresponding conformal testing method
\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}=(S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t}),\{1\})_{t\geq 1}
is such that: for any
\delta^{\prime}>\delta
with
\delta^{\prime}<1-\alpha
, there exists
C_{0}=C_{0}(\delta^{\prime})
and
T_{0}=T_{0}(\delta^{\prime})
, such that we have for
\rho(t)=(\log t)^{1/2}t^{1/2-\beta}+t^{1-\beta}
,

(i)

IER control: for all
t\geq T_{0}
,
\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}=0\mid S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})=1)\leq\alpha+\delta^{\prime}+C_{0}\rho(t)+C_{0}(\log t)^{\nu_{0}/2}t^{-\nu/2}
.

(ii)

Mimicking the oracle: for all
t\geq T_{0}
,

\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}=1\}}S^{*}(X_{t})}}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}=1\}}S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})\big]+C_{0}\rho(t)+C_{0}(\log t)^{\nu_{0}/2}t^{-\nu/2}+\delta^{\prime}.

(iii)

Approximately dominating all other IER controlling procedures:
for any online testing procedure
(S_{t}(X_{t}),\{1\})_{t\geq 1}
(for which
S_{t}(\cdot)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-measurable) is dominated by
OnlineSCI
in the following sense: if for all
t\geq T_{0}
,
\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}=1\mid S_{t}(X_{t})=1)\leq\alpha+\delta^{\prime}+\eta_{t}
, for some rate
\eta_{t}\to 0
, then for all
t\geq T_{0}

\mathbb{E}\big[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}=1\}}S_{t}(X_{t})\big]\leq\mathbb{E}\big[{\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}=1\}}S^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}(X_{t})\big]+C_{0}\rho(t)+C_{0}(\log t)^{\nu_{0}/2}t^{-\nu/2}+C\delta^{\prime}+C\eta_{t},

(69)

where
C>0
is a constant only depending on
\alpha
(and not on
\delta^{\prime}
).

Corollary
5.4
is proved in §
B.17.2
.
According to Corollary
5.4
, the conformal testing procedure of Algorithm
1
can be seen as a counterpart of the Sun and Cai procedure
(
Sun and Cai, 2007
)
in an online testing context, that mimics asymptotically the oracle (with a rate here). In addition, our procedure also provides a control of the FDP in an adversarial setting.
This contrasts with the procedure of
Sun and Cai, 2007
, which operates in the offline batch setting, where the states of the null hypotheses remain unknown and FDP control is therefore not possible.

5.4
Rate and optimality for selection by prediction

Consider the iid regression model (
31
) with
\sigma(x)=1
for simplicity.

Jin and Candes, 2023
are concerned with testing the null hypothesis
Y_{t}\leq y_{0}
(for pre-specified
y_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
) in order to discover
Y_{t}>y_{0}
. We consider this task in an online fashion, by applying the testing example (§
5.3
) with
\tilde{Y}_{t}={\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}<y_{0}\}}
and specific estimates in the adaptive score.

More specifically, we use
OnlineSCI
with the prediction set rule
\mathcal{C}_{t}(x,q)=(y_{0},+\infty)
(so that the non-coverage error is
\mathrm{err}_{t}(y,C)={\mathbf{1}\{y\leq y_{0}\}}
) and the selection rule is (
5
) with the test statistics

\displaystyle W_{t}(x)

\displaystyle:=1-\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y_{0}-\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)}}\right),

(70)

where
\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)
is an
\mathcal{F}_{t-1}
-estimate of
\mu(x)=\mathbb{E}(Y\mid X=x)
.
Hence,
OnlineSCI
and Algorithm
1
with
\tilde{Y}_{t}={\mathbf{1}\{Y_{t}<y_{0}\}}
gives rise to a new online algorithm for selection by prediction.

We can derive convergence properties of this new procedure by applying Theorem
5.3
and Corollary
5.4
in this particular setting.
For this, we should check Assumption
5
with the test statistics
W(x):=1-{\mathrm{lfdr}}(x)
. The local fdr (
64
) is defined in this framework by

\displaystyle{\mathrm{lfdr}}(x)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{P}(Y\leq y_{0}\mid X=x)=F\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y_{0}-{\mu}(x)}}\right),

(71)

where
F
is the cdf of the noise
\xi
of the regression model.

Assumption 6
.

The online iid regression model (
31
) is such that
\mathcal{X}=[0,1]^{d}
,
\sigma(x)=1
,
x\in\mathcal{X}
, the noise
\xi
has a continuous density which is positive on the interior of its support and the regression function

\mu(\cdot)
is a Lipschitz function. In addition,
\mu(X)
has a density supported in
[\mu_{\min},\mu_{\max}]
, which is positive on
(\mu_{\min},\mu_{\max})
, where
-\infty<\min_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mu(x)\leq\mu_{\min}<\mu_{\max}\leq\max_{x\in\mathcal{X}}\mu(x)<+\infty
.

Under Assumption
6
, it is clear that Assumption
5
holds with
\kappa=1-F(y_{0}-\mu_{\min})\geq 0
and
B^{*}=1-F(y_{0}-\mu_{\max})\leq 1
(hence
\alpha^{*}=F(y_{0}-\mu_{\max})
). In addition, by considering a classical kernel-based lfdr estimate
\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}(x)
of
\mathrm{lfdr}(x)
(not using the explicit expression (
71
) because
F
is unknown), we can meet assumption
(
66
) for
\nu_{0}=1
and
\nu=1/(2+d)

(
Tsybakov, 2009
)
. Applying Theorem
5.3
and Corollary
5.4
gives the convergence rate
(\log t)^{1/2}t^{1/2-\beta}+t^{\beta-1}+t^{-1/(4+2d)}
with optimality. Here, the IER can be interpreted has an instantaneous false discovery rate control, while the optimality in (
69
) is in term of instantaneous true discovery rate. Here, note that a criticality phenomenon typically occurs, that is,
\alpha^{*}>0
, for instance when the noise
\xi
as a density supported in
\mathbb{R}
, as in the Gaussian case. Hence, not all error level
\alpha>0
can be reached for selection by prediction.

Remark 5.1

(Predictive lower bound)
.

Our theory can also be applied in this context to add a (non trivial) lower-bound

\hat{\mu}_{t}(X_{t})+\Phi^{-1}(1-q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}})>y_{0}

on
Y_{t}
when
Y_{t}
is declared to be above
y_{0}
. For this,
OnlineSCI
algorithm is applied with
the score function
V_{t}(x,y)=1-\Phi\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(y-\hat{\mu}_{t}(x)}}\right)
(and the corresponding prediction set (
3
), which is thus different than above) and test statistic
W_{t}(x)=V_{t}(x,y_{0})
(same as in (
70
)), see Algorithm
3
.
Under assumptions similar to those in §
4
for the regression model and estimators, we can obtain an instantaneous false coverage rate control at a rate analogue to
\rho(t)
above.
It is interesting to note that since
y_{0}
is always smaller than the predicted lower bound, we have

\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\leq y_{0}\>|\>S_{t}(X_{t})=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1})\leq\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\notin[\hat{\mu}_{t}(X_{t})+\Phi^{-1}(1-q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}),+\infty)\>|\>S_{t}(X_{t})=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}).

Hence, even if it was not built for this in the first place, this method also controls the instantaneous error rate in the sense of false discoveries. Nevertheless, the approached threshold
q^{0}_{\alpha}
with this method is not the same than the optimal one given above. Hence, the additional information (lower-bound) provided by this method is not free; it comes at the price of a stricter selection.

6
Numerical experiments

6.1
Synthetic data

In this section, we evaluate our method on synthetic data for the different applications presented above.
In a nutshell, we show that the method of
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
fails to control the FCP over the selected examples and that the inflation in FCP can be large; we demonstrate the actual convergence of
OnlineSCI
in a manner which is in accordance with the proved theoretical results; we show the large potential advantage of using adaptive scores over non-adaptive scores with regard to overall and instantaneous power.

6.1.1
Online conformal testing

For online testing (see Section
5.3
), we consider the following iid data generation. Examples are sampled from the mixture model

\displaystyle Y\sim\text{Bernoulli}(0.2)\quad\text{and}\quad(X\mid Y=y)\sim\mathcal{N}((0,0),I_{2})\cdot{\mathbf{1}\{y=0\}}+\mathcal{N}((3,3),I_{2})\cdot{\mathbf{1}\{y=1\}}\;,

where
I_{2}
is the identity matrix of size
2\times 2
,
Y=0
corresponds to a null and
Y=1
to an alternative. In this specific application, our goal is to identify the observations
X_{t}
associated to
Y_{t}=1
.

We apply Algorithm
3
with
\alpha=0.1
,
q_{1}=0.5
and
\gamma_{t}=t^{-3/4}
(as suggested by Remark
3.2
), and compute an estimator
\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}
of
\mathrm{lfdr}
(
64
)
by using a Random Forest (RF) classifier with the default parameters in
scikit-learn

(
Pedregosa et al., 2011
)
. At each time, the estimation is performed in two different ways:

1.

Adaptive statistic:
the RF estimator is updated at each time
t
with the new available pair
(X_{t-1},Y_{t-1})
;

2.

Fixed statistic:
the RF estimator is trained with an independent data set
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
of size
100
.

Figure 3
:
Online conformal testing: results for
OnlineSCI
and the oracle policy on a particular data set. Top left panel:
\mathrm{FDP}_{t}
versus time step
t
. Top right panel: method threshold
q_{t}
versus time step
t
. Bottom left and right panel:
\mathrm{IER}_{t}
and instantaneous power at each time step.

Figure 4
:
Online conformal testing with adaptive
OnlineSCI
: same as Figure
3
top panels with

5
different data set generations from the same model.

Finally, the following benchmark procedures are also considered:

•

Oracle policy: Algorithm
3
with
W_{t}(x)=1-\mathrm{lfdr}(x)=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y=1\mid X=x}}\right)
. It is considered as the best benchmark procedure, because it achieves both FCP control and threshold convergence to the optimal solution of
Sun and Cai, 2007
. Recall that this is not the case for the procedure with constant threshold
q^{0}_{\alpha}
, which has no finite sample FCP bound guarantee.

•

Levels based On Recent Discovery (LORD)
(
Javanmard and Montanari, 2015
)
: At each time step
t
, we construct a conformal
p
-value with the score
V_{t}(x,0)=1-\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}(x)
and where only the
(X_{s})_{1\leq s\leq t}
associated to
Y_{s}=0
are considered. We consider the version
3
of LORD therein and we fix the wealth and reward parameters of LORD to
0.08
and
0.02
. Note that here the
p
-values are not independent and so LORD is not guaranteed to control the online FDR.

Finally, for each procedure, we evaluate the
\mathrm{FCP}
(which is the FDP in that case),
\mathrm{IER}
, and the instantaneous power, that is,

\mbox{IPower}_{t}:=\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(S_{t}(X_{t})=1,Y_{t}=1\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}}\right)=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Y_{t}S_{t}(X_{t})\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}}}\right]\leq\mathbb{P}(Y_{1}=1)=0.2.

(72)

This power notion is in accordance with our optimality results, see Section
5
.

Results:

Figure
3
displays the results obtained using (a)
OnlineSCI
with both adaptive and fixed statistics, and (b) the oracle policy. We see that the FDP (top left panel) converges to the nominal level
\alpha=0.1
for all methods, as provided by the theory. In addition, the sequence
(q_{t})_{t\geq 1}
for
OnlineSCI
with adaptive scores (top right panel; blue curve) converges to
q^{0}_{\alpha}
. This behavior is not observed for fixed score (top right panel; orange curve). This was expected as, in this case, the sequence
(q_{t})_{t\geq 1}
converges to the solution of

\mathbb{P}\big(Y_{t}=0\mid 1-\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}(X_{t})>q,{\mathcal{D}}_{0}\big)=\alpha

where
\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{{\mathcal{D}}_{0}}
is computed with
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
, and this solution is not necessary equal to
q^{0}_{\alpha}
. We also clearly see that
OnlineSCI
with adaptive scores returns comparable results as the oracle policy with
\mathrm{IER}_{t}
close to
\alpha
(bottom left panel) and a similar power (bottom right panel). In addition, the convergence of the FDP is faster than the convergence of
q_{t}
, which is reasonable because the setting is iid and we expect that the FDP concentrates better in this favorable case. Figure
4
shows that this is not specific to one data generation.
Finally, LORD makes no discovery in this particular example. This is not surprising, as LORD is designed to operate under much more difficult conditions than those considered in this paper.

6.1.2
Selective classification with
2
classes

For online selective classification (see Section
5.2
), we consider the following iid data generation: examples are sampled from the mixture model

\displaystyle Y=\text{Bernoulli}(0.5)\quad\text{and}\quad(X\mid Y=y)\sim\mathcal{N}((0,0),I_{2})\cdot{\mathbf{1}\{y=0\}}+\mathcal{N}((1,1),I_{2})\cdot{\mathbf{1}\{y=1\}}\;.

We apply Algorithm
2
with
\alpha=0.1
,
q_{1}=0.8
and
\gamma_{t}=0.5\cdot t^{-3/4}
and compute the estimator
V_{t}(x,y)
in (
54
) using a Gaussian Bayes Naive (GBN) classifier with the default parameters in
scikit-learn

(
Pedregosa et al., 2011
)
. As in the previous section, the learning is performed in two different ways:

1.

Adaptive statistic:
the GBN estimator is updated at each time
t
with the new available pair
(X_{t-1},Y_{t-1})
;

2.

Fixed statistic:
the GBN estimator is trained with an independent data set
{\mathcal{D}}_{0}
of size
10
.

The benchmark procedures are:

•

The oracle policy, which is Algorithm
2
for which the true probabilities are used instead of their estimates.

•

ACI with decaying step sizes
(
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
)
where the non-conformity score is
1-\widehat{\pi}_{t}(x,y)
(estimator of
\mathbb{P}(Y_{t}\neq y\>|\>X_{t}=x)
).

Results:

Figure
5
illustrates the results obtained with the different algorithms. As expected, the
\mathrm{FCP}
is controlled at level
\alpha=0.1
for all methods except ACI of
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
with naive selection (top left panel), which has a much larger FCP. Furthermore, the threshold sequences
(q_{t})_{t\geq 1}
are all converging, but with different limits; only the
OnlineSCI
with adaptive score (blue curve) and the oracle policy (green curve) have thresholds converging to
q^{0}_{\alpha}
. Figure
5
(bottom right panel) also shows that the adaptive method strongly dominates the non-adaptive one in terms of instantaneous power.

6.2
Real data: online conformal testing and selective classification for
Cifar10
data set

We use the image data set
Cifar10
(
https://www.cs.toronto.edu/~kriz/cifar.html
), which consists of
60000

32\times 32
colour images in
10
classes, with
6000
images per class.

We consider two applications: online conformal testing and online selective classification.

1.

For online conformal testing, the null classes are ‘
plane
’ and ‘
truck
’, and the alternative is ‘
cat
’. The total number of images in the hold-out set
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and in the online stream of data are equal to
15000
and
3000
, respectively. Using the hold-out set, we compute
\widehat{\mathrm{lfdr}}_{t}
with a convolutional Neural Network (CNN) with 2 convolutional layers, one pooling layer, and 3 fully-connected trained using a cross-entropy loss and a stochastic gradient descent with a learning rate of
0.001
and a momentum equal to
0.9
. As in the synthetic section, we compare ourself to LORD (version 3 in
Javanmard and Montanari, 2015
).

2.

For online selective classification, we consider the classes ‘
deer
’ and ‘
cat
’. The total number of images in the hold-out and online stream of data are equal to
10000
and
2000
, respectively. We estimate
\mathbb{P}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y=y\mid X=x}}\right)
with a CNN with 2 convolutional layers, one pooling layer, and 3 fully-connected trained using a cross-entropy loss and a stochastic gradient descent with a learning rate of
0.001
and a momentum equal to
0.9
. As in the synthetic section, we compare ourself to ACI
(
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
)
where we select if the returned prediction set at time
t
is of size one.

Results:

Figure
7
displays, for the two applications, the
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}
(left panel) and the sequence
(q_{t})_{t\geq 1}
(right panel) returned by
OnlineSCI
(and by ACI for selective classification). We see that
OnlineSCI
controls the
\mathrm{FCP}
at the nominal level
\alpha
(blue curves) in both cases, which is not the case of ACI in the case of online selective classification (red curve). As in the synthetic data, LORD is not able to make discovery; the first
p
-values are not accurate enough, as we do not have enough data at these time points.

7
Discussion

In this work, we introduced the method
OnlineSCI
which allows to make various online selective conformal inferences. We show both theoretically and empirically that it makes the best of two worlds: while it comes with an FCP bound guarantee over all possible sequences of observations, it controls the instantaneous error rate at a level close to
\alpha
when the time gets large in the favorable case where the data are identically distributed (iid or autoregressive) and under mild assumptions. At this moment, we are not aware of any other method achieving both tasks together: on the one hand, the (generally unknown) method using the constant oracle threshold
q^{0}_{\alpha}
does not provide the FCP bound guarantee in the adversarial setting. On the other hand, the selective DtACI method of
Bao et al., 2024b
is not proved to control the instantaneous error rate in the iid setting. Finally, in our leading cases (iid online regression, selective classification, conformal testing), we establish that the method
OnlineSCI
satisfies an optimality property, up to remainder terms that vanish at specific rates.

However, for informative selections, we have shown that a theoretical failing probability
\delta>0
comes into play in our IER bounds, which records the unfavorable trajectories of
OnlineSCI
for which the initial parameters of the algorithm have been badly chosen and the algorithm is ’frozen’. The error
\delta
can be reduced by restarting the algorithm and adjusting the parameter choice if necessary. We show that this is the price to pay for having the adversarial FCP bound (Proposition
C.1
).

This works opened several interesting avenues for future research.
For non-adaptive scores, our method relies only on the observed outcomes at the selected time points. This is particularly desirable in applications where measuring the outcome is costly. For instance, in health screening, individuals might undergo further diagnostic testing only if selected by an online conformal testing procedure, where the null hypothesis is that the person is healthy. Online conformal testing remains applicable even when the health status of unselected individuals is unknown. An interesting direction for future work is to move beyond fixed scores by developing adaptive scores that incorporate evidence from the covariates, which are observed at all time points.

Our convergence rates also provided guidelines to calibrate the step sequence
\gamma_{t}
in
OnlineSCI
(Remark
3.2
). While this practical recommendations seem to be efficient in practice, the picture is far from being complete, and it is interesting to explore other ways to set this step sequence, for instance by using part of the past data. This is a very active area research in the corresponding machine learning literature
which promises interesting future developments and recommendations for
OnlineSCI
.

Appendix A
General approximation results

In this section, we quantify non-asymptotically how the threshold
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}
can get close to a threshold
q^{0}_{\alpha}
that makes an instantaneous error rate close to
\alpha
when
t
grows. In particular, this shows that
\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})
can approach
\alpha
up to remainder terms that is meant to vanish when
t
is large (see Lemma
A.4
below).

First, we relax Assumption
1
.

Assumption 7
.

The benchmark function
\Pi^{0}:q\in[0,B]\mapsto\Pi^{0}(q)\in[0,1]
is a deterministic function such that for there exists a value
q^{0}_{\alpha}\in(0,B)
with
\Pi^{0}(q^{0}_{\alpha})=\alpha
and for all
q\in[0,q^{0}_{\alpha})
,
\Pi^{0}(q)>\alpha
, for all
q\in(q^{0}_{\alpha},B]
,
\Pi^{0}(q)<\alpha
.
In addition, there exists a compact
[\ell_{\alpha},u_{\alpha}]\subset(0,1)
such that
\ell_{\alpha}<q^{0}_{\alpha}<u_{\alpha}
,
\inf_{q\in(0,\ell_{\alpha})}\Pi^{0}(q)>\alpha
and
\sup_{q\in(u_{\alpha},B)}\Pi^{0}(q)<\alpha
.

Typically, the first statement of Assumption
7
is satisfied if
\Pi^{0}
is continuous decreasing on
[\ell_{\alpha},u_{\alpha}]
and
\Pi^{0}(q^{0}_{\alpha})=\alpha
.
The last statement of Assumption
7
is satisfied if
\Pi^{0}
is either non-increasing on
[0,1]
, or continuous over
(0,1)
with
\lim_{0^{+}}\Pi^{0}>\alpha
and
\lim_{B^{-}}\Pi^{0}<\alpha
. In these cases, we can choose
\ell_{\alpha}=q^{0}_{\alpha}/2
and
u_{\alpha}=(q^{0}_{\alpha}+B)/2
.
Finally, the slope of
\Pi^{0}
on the vicinity of
q^{0}_{\alpha}
is a useful quantity to obtain convergence rate, and we let

\displaystyle a

\displaystyle:=\inf\Big\{\frac{|\Pi^{0}(q)-\alpha|}{|q-q^{0}_{\alpha}|}\>:\>q\in[\ell_{\alpha},u_{\alpha}],\Pi^{0}(q)\neq\alpha\Big\}\geq 0;

(73)

\displaystyle b

\displaystyle:=\sup\Big\{\frac{|\Pi^{0}(q)-\alpha|}{|q-q^{0}_{\alpha}|}\>:\>q\in[\ell_{\alpha},u_{\alpha}],q\neq q^{0}_{\alpha}\Big\}\leq+\infty.

(74)

Then, the minimum/maximum slope of
\Pi^{0}
wrt
q^{0}_{\alpha}
can be quantified by introducing

\displaystyle\tilde{a}

\displaystyle:=a\wedge\frac{\inf_{q\in(0,\ell_{\alpha})}\Pi^{0}(q)-\alpha}{B}\wedge\frac{\alpha-\sup_{q\in(u_{\alpha},B)}\Pi^{0}(q)}{B}\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{1-\alpha}{\alpha\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}}}}\right)\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\alpha}{B+(1-\alpha)\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}}}}\right);

(75)

\displaystyle\tilde{b}

\displaystyle:=b\vee\frac{1-\alpha}{q^{0}_{\alpha}-\ell_{\alpha}}\vee\frac{\alpha}{u_{\alpha}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}.

(76)

To derive rates we also consider the following mild assumption:

Assumption 8
.

The constant
a
(
73
) is positive and the constant
b
(
74
) is finite.

The following result is obvious.

Lemma A.1
.

Assumptions
7
and
8
are both satisfied under Assumption
1
.

Our first non-asymptotical bound is as follows.

Theorem A.2
.

[In-probability bound]
Consider the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
with any nonincreasing step sequence
(\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
.
Consider some function
\Pi^{0}
satisfying Assumption
7
with some
q^{0}_{\alpha}\in(0,B)
and the corresponding
D_{t}
being defined by (
20
). Recall that
J(t)=\sum_{k=1}^{t-1}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})+1
is given by (
13
).
Let for all
T>t\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\epsilon_{t,T}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma_{J(k)}(\mathrm{err}_{k}(Y_{k},{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}))-\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{k},S_{k}}(q_{k}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}))\cdot{\mathbf{1}\{q_{k}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\geq 0\}}\cdot S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}).

(77)

Then
for all
T>t\geq 1
, we have point-wise

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{T}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq\>

\displaystyle(\max_{t\leq k\leq T}D_{k})/\tilde{a}+\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}|+\gamma_{J(t)}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}}}\right)^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\epsilon_{t-1,T}}}\right\rvert+B+\gamma_{1}}}\right),

(78)

with the convention
1/0=+\infty
if
\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}=0
or
\tilde{a}=0
.
In addition, the process
(\epsilon_{t,T})_{T\geq t}
vanishes in the following sense: for all
x,y>0
,
T\geq t
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}\Big(\epsilon_{t,T}>x\Big)\leq\mathbb{P}\bigg(\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}^{2}>y\bigg)+e^{-2x^{2}/y}.

(79)

The proof is given in §
B.2
, in a slightly more general context where
\Pi^{0}
is allowed to depend on
t
.

Let us provide some intuition for the bound given in (
78
).
First, while the bound is on the term
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{T}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert
, it is valid for any
t<T
and the quality of the bound depend on the choice of
t
. For instance, we can take
t=t(T)=T/2
, but other choices can lead to better bound (see, e.g., Theorem
3.2
case (ii)). Second, the process
\epsilon_{t-1,T}
is guaranteed to be small by (
79
), when
\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}^{2}
is not too large, which is true if we choose a step sequence such that
\sum_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}^{2}<\infty
and if the procedure makes enough selections when
t,T
are large.
Third,
\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}
should be large, which is true if
\sum_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}=\infty
and
J(T+1)
is large. Hence, it is natural to choose a step sequence with (
21
).
Fourth,
D_{k}
should be small in order to obtain a small bound.

Our second non-asymptotical bound is as follows.

Theorem A.3
.

[In-expectation bound]
Consider the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
with any starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
and a nonincreasing step sequence
(\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
. Consider some function
\Pi^{0}
satisfying Assumption
7
with some
q^{0}_{t,\alpha}\in(0,B)
and the corresponding
D_{t}
being defined by (
20
). Consider
\tilde{a}
given by (
75
) and
J(\cdot)
given by (
13
).
Assume that for some
t\geq j_{0}\geq 1
,

(i)

1-2\tilde{a}\gamma_{j_{0}}\geq 0
and
\gamma_{j}/\gamma_{j+1}\leq 1/(1-\tilde{a}\gamma_{j})
for
j_{0}\leq j\leq t
;

(ii)

S_{k}
is independent of the past
\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
, for
j_{0}\leq k\leq t
.

Then, for any
t_{0}
with
j_{0}\leq t_{0}\leq t
with
J(t_{0})\geq j_{0}
, we have pointwise

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1}\dots S_{t-1}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma_{J(t)}}{\gamma_{J(t_{0})}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})^{2}\vee\frac{4}{\tilde{a}}}}\right)+4(B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})\gamma_{J(t)}\sum_{k=t_{0}}^{t-1}S_{k}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[D_{k}\mid S_{1}\dots S_{k-1}}}\right],

(80)

where we denoted
S_{t}=S_{t}(X_{t},q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t})
for all
t\geq 1
.

Theorem
A.3
is proved in §
B.4
.
This bound is simpler than the one obtained in Theorem
A.2
, but relies on a stronger independence assumption (ii). Assumption (ii) is satisfied typically when the
(X_{t},Y_{t})
are independent over time and the selection rule only involve
X_{t}
(
X
-oriented selection). To make the bound in (
80
) small, the sequence
\gamma_{j}
should vanishes while
J(t)
should grows to infinity when
t
grows. The other remainder term is small when
D_{t}
is small enough. Finally, the assumption (i) is relatively light, and is satisfied for the sequence
\gamma_{j}=cj^{-\beta}
for
\beta\in(0,1)
. Note that the two conditions in (
21
) are not required here.

Finally, bounding
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert
directly leads to a bound on the
\mathrm{IER}
.

Lemma A.4
.

Consider the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
with any nonincreasing step sequence
(\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
.
Consider some function
\Pi^{0}
satisfying Assumption
7
with some
q^{0}_{t,\alpha}\in(0,B)
and the corresponding
D_{t}
being defined by (
20
).
Considering in addition
\tilde{b}
as in (
76
). Then we have for all
t\geq 1
, provided that
q_{t}^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}\in[0,B]
,

|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha|\leq D_{t}+\tilde{b}|q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q_{\alpha}^{0}|.

(81)

Lemma
A.4
is proved in §
B.5
. It is typically combined with the bounds above to obtain bounds on
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\mathrm{IER}_{t}(\mathcal{R}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\alpha}}\right\rvert
.

Appendix B
Proofs

B.1
Proofs for §
3

Proof of Lemma
2.1

Let us first remark that if
q_{t}<0
, then
q_{t+1}\geq q_{t}
and if
q_{t}>B
, then there is two possible result. Either there is no selection, and in that case
q_{t+1}=q_{t}
, either there is a selection and there is no mistake, and in that case
q_{t+1}\leq q_{t}
. Hence, for any
t\geq 1
,
q_{t+1}\geq\gamma_{t}(\mathrm{react}_{t}(X_{t},Y_{t},q_{t})-\alpha)\geq-\max_{1\leq j\leq J(t)}(\gamma_{j})\cdot\alpha
and
q_{t+1}\leq B+\gamma_{t}(\mathrm{react}_{t}(X_{t},Y_{t},q_{t})-\alpha)\leq B+\max_{1\leq j\leq J(t)}(\gamma_{j})\cdot(1-\alpha)
.

Proof of Theorem
2.2

We follow
Angelopoulos et al., 2024
(Theorem 2 therein), but restricting only to selected indices. We have by definition of
\mathrm{FCP}_{t}
that, for all
t\geq 1
and
J:=J(t)
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{FCP}_{t}(\mathcal{R})-\alpha

\displaystyle=J^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{J}(\mathrm{err}_{\tau_{k}}(X_{\tau_{k}},Y_{\tau_{k}},q_{\tau_{k}})-\alpha)

\displaystyle\leq J^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{J}(\mathrm{react}_{\tau_{k}}(X_{\tau_{k}},Y_{\tau_{k}},q_{\tau_{k}})-\alpha),

where we used that
\mathrm{err}_{t}(x,y,q)\leq\mathrm{react}_{t}(x,y,q)
for all
x,y,q
and
\tau_{k}
denotes the time at which we have made the
k
-th selection. Then the last display is

\displaystyle J^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{J}\gamma^{-1}_{k}[\gamma_{k}(\mathrm{react}_{\tau_{k}}(X_{\tau_{k}},Y_{\tau_{k}},q_{\tau_{k}})-\alpha)]

\displaystyle=J^{-1}\sum_{k=1}^{J}\sum_{j=1}^{k}(\gamma^{-1}_{j}-\gamma^{-1}_{j-1})[\gamma_{k}(\mathrm{react}_{\tau_{k}}(X_{\tau_{k}},Y_{\tau_{k}},q_{\tau_{k}})-\alpha)]

\displaystyle=J^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{J}(\gamma^{-1}_{j}-\gamma^{-1}_{j-1})\sum_{k=j}^{J}[\gamma_{k}(\mathrm{react}_{k}(X_{\tau_{k}},Y_{\tau_{k}},q_{\tau_{k}})-\alpha)]

\displaystyle=J^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{J}(\gamma^{-1}_{j}-\gamma^{-1}_{j-1})(q_{\tau_{J}}-q_{\tau_{j}}).

Hence we obtain

\displaystyle\mathrm{FCP}_{t}(\mathcal{R})-\alpha

\displaystyle\leq\big\lvert J^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{J}(\gamma^{-1}_{j}-\gamma^{-1}_{j-1})(q_{\tau_{J}}-q_{\tau_{j}})\big\rvert

\displaystyle\leq J^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lvert\gamma^{-1}_{j}-\gamma^{-1}_{j-1}\rvert\lvert q_{\tau_{J}}-q_{\tau_{j}}\rvert\;,

where
\gamma^{-1}_{0}=0
by convention. Now, using Lemma
2.1
, we have
\lvert q_{\tau_{J}}-q_{\tau_{j}}\rvert\leq(B+\max_{1\leq j\leq J(t)}(\gamma_{j}))
and thus

\displaystyle\mathrm{FCP}_{t}(\mathcal{R})-\alpha

\displaystyle\leq(B+\max_{1\leq j\leq J(t)}(\gamma_{j}))J^{-1}\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lvert\gamma^{-1}_{j}-\gamma^{-1}_{j-1}\rvert

\displaystyle=\dfrac{(B+\max_{1\leq j\leq J(t)}(\gamma_{j}))}{J}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\gamma^{-1}_{1}+\sum_{j=2}^{J}\lvert\gamma^{-1}_{j}-\gamma^{-1}_{j-1}\rvert}}\right)\;.

This gives the first FCP upper-bound. When
(\gamma_{t})_{t\geq 1}
is nondecreasing,
\sum_{j=1}^{J}\lvert\gamma^{-1}_{j}-\gamma^{-1}_{j-1}\rvert=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\gamma^{-1}_{j}-\gamma^{-1}_{j-1}=\gamma^{-1}_{J}
and we obtain the second FCP upper-bound.

B.2
Proof of Theorem
A.2

We prove Theorem
A.2
by proving a slightly more general result, for which
\Pi^{0}_{t}
may depend on time.

B.2.1
Extending Theorem
A.2

We extend Theorem
A.2
to a more general situation where the benchmark function
\Pi^{0}_{t}
possibly depends on the time
t
. Hence,
q^{0}_{\alpha}
,
a
and
\tilde{a}
now depend on
t
, and
D_{t}
reads
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\Pi^{0}_{t}(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})}}\right\rvert
.
For simplicity, and with some abuse of notation, the numbers of the equations are unchanged when we refer to that quantities. For instance, we still refer to (
20
) for
D_{t}
, even if
\Pi^{0}_{t}
depends on
t
.

Theorem B.1
.

Consider the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
with any step sequence
(\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
and starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
. For each
t\geq 1
, consider some function
\Pi^{0}_{t}
satisfying Assumption
7
with some
q^{0}_{t,\alpha}\in(0,B)
and the corresponding
D_{t}
in (
20
). Let
a_{t}\geq 0
and
\tilde{a}_{t}\geq 0
being defined by (
73
) and (
75
), respectively, in which
\Pi^{0}(q)
and
q^{0}_{\alpha}
have been replaced by
\Pi^{0}_{t}(q)
and
q^{0}_{t,\alpha}
, respectively.
Then for all
T>t\geq 1
, we have point-wise

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{T}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\max_{t\leq k\leq T}(D_{k}/\tilde{a}_{k})+\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}|+\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}|+\max_{J(t)\leq j\leq J(T-1)}\gamma_{j}

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\min_{t\leq k\leq T}\tilde{a}_{k}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}}}\right)^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\epsilon_{t-1,T}}}\right\rvert+B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}}}\right)}}\right],

(82)

with the convention
1/0=+\infty
if
\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}=0
and the process defined by
(\epsilon_{t,T})_{T\geq t}
(
77
) satisfying (
79
).

Theorem
B.1
is proved in §
B.2.2
.
Compared to the original Theorem
A.2
, the bound in Theorem
B.1
essentially contains the additional term
\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}|
measuring how
\Pi^{0}_{k}
(hence
q^{0}_{k,\alpha}
) moves when
k
varies from
t
to
T-1
. It is zero when
\Pi^{0}_{k}
does not depend on
k
, and we recover Theorem
A.2
.
Another difference is that the sequence
(\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
is non necessarily assumed to be nonincreasing for the sake of generality.

B.2.2
Proof of Theorem
B.1

Let us denote
q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}
by
q_{t}
for short.
First consider for a given
t\geq 1
the functions

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(q)

\displaystyle:=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{react}_{t}(Y_{t},{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q))\>|\>S_{t}(X_{t},q)=1,\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]=\Pi_{t}(q){\mathbf{1}\{q\in[0,B]\}}+{\mathbf{1}\{q<0\}},\>\>q\in\mathbb{R};

(83)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}^{0}(q)

\displaystyle:=\Pi^{0}_{t}(q){\mathbf{1}\{q\in[0,B]\}}+{\mathbf{1}\{q<0\}},\>\>q\in\mathbb{R},

(84)

where
\Pi_{t}(q):=\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{t},S_{t}}(q)
is given by (
18
).
Note that
D_{t}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\Pi_{t}(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\Pi^{0}_{t}(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})}}\right\rvert{\mathbf{1}\{q^{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}_{t}\in[0,B]\}}
given by (
20
) is also such that

D_{t}=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})-\widetilde{\Pi}^{0}_{t}(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})}}\right\rvert,\>\>t\geq 1.

(85)

Also, by Assumption
7
for
\Pi^{0}_{t}
and with
\tilde{a}_{t}
corresponding to definition (
75
), we have

\inf_{t\geq 1}\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}^{0}(q_{t})-\alpha}}\right\rvert}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{t}-q^{0}_{t,\alpha}}}\right\rvert}\geq\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\frac{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}^{0}(q)-\alpha}}\right\rvert}{\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q-q^{0}_{t,\alpha}}}\right\rvert},-\alpha\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}\leq q\leq B+(1-\alpha)\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}}}\right\}\geq\tilde{a}_{t}.

(86)

Lastly, our proof rely on the following quantities, for
\epsilon\in(0,\alpha\wedge(1-\alpha))
,

\displaystyle q^{0}_{t,\alpha+\epsilon}

\displaystyle:=\inf\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{q\in\mathbb{R}\>:\>\widetilde{\Pi}^{0}_{t}(q)\leq\alpha+\epsilon}}\right\};

(87)

\displaystyle q^{0}_{t,\alpha-\epsilon}

\displaystyle:=\sup\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{q\in\mathbb{R}\>:\>\widetilde{\Pi}^{0}_{t}(q)\geq\alpha-\epsilon}}\right\}.

(88)

Note that by Assumption
7
for
\Pi^{0}_{t}
, these quantities always exist and we have
-\alpha\sup_{j}\gamma_{j}/2\leq q^{0}_{t,\alpha+\epsilon}\leq q^{0}_{t,\alpha}
and
B\geq q^{0}_{t,\alpha-\epsilon}\geq q^{0}_{t,\alpha}
. In addition, we also have the relations:

\displaystyle q^{0}_{t,\alpha-\epsilon}-q^{0}_{t,\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon/\tilde{a}_{t};

(89)

\displaystyle q^{0}_{t,\alpha}-q^{0}_{t,\alpha+\epsilon}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon/\tilde{a}_{t}.

(90)

Indeed, let us establish (
90
) (the proof for (
89
) is similar) by assuming
q^{0}_{t,\alpha}>q^{0}_{t,\alpha+\epsilon}
(otherwise there is nothing to prove). By (
87
), there exists a sequence
(\eta_{s})_{s\geq 1}>0
tending to zero, such that for large
s
,
{\Pi}^{0}_{t}(q^{0}_{t,\alpha+\epsilon}+\eta_{s})\leq\alpha+\epsilon
. Hence, by (
86
), we have

q^{0}_{t,\alpha}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{0}_{t,\alpha+\epsilon}+\eta_{s}}}\right)=\frac{q^{0}_{t,\alpha}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{0}_{t,\alpha+\epsilon}+\eta_{s}}}\right)}{{\Pi}^{0}_{t}(q^{0}_{t,\alpha+\epsilon}+\eta_{s})-\alpha}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left({\Pi}^{0}_{t}(q^{0}_{t,\alpha+\epsilon}+\eta_{s})-\alpha}}\right)\leq\epsilon/\tilde{a}_{t}.

Hence we obtain (
90
) by taking
s
tending to infinity.

Now that these notation have been introduced, we prove Theorem
B.1
. We obtain by (
12
) that for
T\geq 1
,

\displaystyle q_{T+1}-q_{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=1}^{T}(q_{t+1}-q_{t})=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\gamma_{J(t)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{react}_{t}(Y_{t},{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}),q_{t})-\alpha}}\right)\cdot S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}).

(91)

Considering the process

\displaystyle Z_{T}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{t=1}^{T}\gamma_{J(t)}(\mathrm{react}_{t}(Y_{t},{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}),q_{t})-\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(q_{t}))\cdot S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}),\>\>\>T\geq 1,

(92)

we have

q_{T+1}-q_{1}=Z_{T}+\sum_{t=1}^{T}\gamma_{J(t)}(\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(q_{t})-\alpha)\cdot S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t})
.
This entails, for
1\leq t<T
,

\displaystyle q_{T+1}-q_{t}=Z_{T}-Z_{t-1}

\displaystyle+\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma_{J(k)}(\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})-\alpha)\cdot S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})

As a result, by (
77
),
Z_{T-1}-Z_{t-1}=\epsilon_{t-1,T-1}
and we have for any
1\leq t<T
,

\displaystyle q_{T}-q_{t}

\displaystyle=\epsilon_{t-1,T-1}+\sum_{k=t}^{T-1}\gamma_{J(k)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})-\alpha}}\right)\cdot S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}).

(93)

Now fix any
1\leq t<T
and consider the set of “crossing-point times”

\mathcal{C}=\{t\leq k\leq T-1\>:\>\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})\leq\alpha<\widetilde{\Pi}_{k+1}(q_{k+1})\mbox{ or }\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})>\alpha\geq\widetilde{\Pi}_{k+1}(q_{k+1})\}

Case 1:
\mathcal{C}
is not empty

In this case, we may let
t^{*}=\max\mathcal{C}
.
By definition, we have either of the two following cases:

(i)

\widetilde{\Pi}_{t^{*}}(q_{t^{*}})\leq\alpha\mbox{ and }\forall s\in\{t^{*}+1,\dots,T\},\widetilde{\Pi}_{s}(q_{s})>\alpha
;

(ii)

\widetilde{\Pi}_{t^{*}}(q_{t^{*}})>\alpha\mbox{ and }\forall s\in\{t^{*}+1,\dots,T\},\widetilde{\Pi}_{s}(q_{s})\leq\alpha
.

In case (i), we have by (
93
) that

q_{T}-q_{t^{*}}\geq\epsilon_{t^{*}-1,T-1}+\gamma_{J(t^{*})}(\widetilde{\Pi}_{t^{*}}(q_{t^{*}})-\alpha)S_{t^{*}}(X_{t^{*}},q_{t^{*}})
, that is,

\gamma_{J(t^{*})}(\alpha-\widetilde{\Pi}_{t^{*}}(q_{t^{*}}))\geq\gamma_{J(t^{*})}(\alpha-\Pi^{0}_{t^{*}}(q_{t^{*}}))S_{t^{*}}(X_{t^{*}},q_{t^{*}})\geq\epsilon_{t^{*}-1,T-1}-(q_{T}-q_{t^{*}})
and since

\widetilde{\Pi}_{T}(q_{T})>\alpha\geq\widetilde{\Pi}_{t^{*}}(q_{t^{*}})
and by (
85
) for
D_{T}
and
D_{t^{*}}
, we then have
{\Pi}^{0}_{T}(q_{T})+D_{T}>\alpha\geq{\Pi}^{0}_{t^{*}}(q_{t^{*}})-D_{t^{*}}
.
By (
87
), this means that
q_{t^{*}}\geq q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha+D_{t^{*}}}
with
q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha+D_{t^{*}}}\leq q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}
.
Thus

\displaystyle q_{t^{*}}-q_{T}\geq q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha+D_{t^{*}}}-q_{T}

\displaystyle=q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha+D_{t^{*}}}-q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}+q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}+q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q_{T}

\displaystyle\geq q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q_{T}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}-q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha+D_{t^{*}}}}}\right)-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}}}\right\rvert.

Similarly, since by (
88
),
q_{T}\leq q^{0}_{T,\alpha-D_{T}}
with
q^{0}_{T,\alpha-D_{T}}\geq q^{0}_{T,\alpha}
, we have

q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}\leq q^{0}_{T,\alpha-D_{T}}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}.

Combining the above bounds leads to

\displaystyle|q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}|\leq\gamma_{J(t^{*})}-\epsilon_{t^{*}-1,T-1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}}}\right\rvert+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}-q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha+D_{t^{*}}}}}\right)\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{0}_{T,\alpha-D_{T}}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}}}\right).

Now, by (
89
) and (
90
), we have
q^{0}_{T,\alpha-D_{T}}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}\leq D_{T}/\tilde{a}_{T}
and
q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}-q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha+D_{t^{*}}}\leq D_{t^{*}}/\tilde{a}_{t^{*}}
respectively.
This provides

\displaystyle|q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}|\leq\max_{J(t)\leq j\leq J(T-1)}\gamma_{j}+\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}|+\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}|+\max_{t\leq k\leq T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{k}/\tilde{a}_{k}}}\right).

(94)

In case (ii), by (
93
), we have similarly

q_{T}-q_{t^{*}}\leq\epsilon_{t^{*}-1,T-1}+\gamma_{J(t^{*})}(\widetilde{\Pi}_{t^{*}}(q_{t^{*}})-\alpha)S_{t^{*}}(X_{t^{*}},q_{t^{*}})\leq\epsilon_{t^{*}-1,T-1}+\gamma_{J(t^{*})}(\widetilde{\Pi}_{t^{*}}(q_{t^{*}})-\alpha)
. Also, by (
85
), and since
\widetilde{\Pi}_{T}(q_{T})-D_{T}\leq\alpha<\widetilde{\Pi}_{t^{*}}(q_{t^{*}})+D_{t^{*}}
in this case, we have both

q_{T}\geq q^{0}_{T,\alpha+D_{T}}
and
q_{t^{*}}\leq q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha-D_{t^{*}}}
and thus

q_{T}-q_{t^{*}}\geq q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}+q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}+q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}-q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha-D_{t^{*}}}\geq q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}}}\right\rvert-\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha-D_{t^{*}}}-q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}}}\right).

In particular,
q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}\leq\epsilon_{t^{*}-1,T-1}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}}}\right\rvert+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha-D_{t^{*}}}-q^{0}_{t^{*},\alpha}}}\right)+\gamma_{J(t^{*})}
.
In addition, since
q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q_{T}\leq q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha+D_{T}}
, this case also gives (
94
) by using (
89
) and (
90
).

Case 2:
\mathcal{C}
is empty.

In this case, we have either of the two following cases:

(i)

\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})\leq\alpha
for all
t\leq k\leq T
;

(ii)

\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})\geq\alpha
for all
t\leq k\leq T
.

In case (i), we have by (
93
) that

q_{T+1}-q_{t}\leq\epsilon_{t-1,T}-\min_{t\leq k\leq T}|\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})-\alpha|\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma_{J(k)}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}).

In addition
|q_{T+1}-q_{t}|\leq B+\max_{1\leq j\leq J(T+1)}(\gamma_{j})
by Lemma
2.1
, hence we have

\min_{t\leq k\leq T}|\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})-\alpha|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma_{J(k)}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})}}\right)^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\epsilon_{t-1,T}|+B+\max_{1\leq j\leq J(T+1)}\gamma_{j}}}\right),

with the convention
1/0=+\infty
if
\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma_{J(k)}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})=0
.
As a result, by (
85
) of
D_{k}
, and since
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\Pi^{0}_{k}(q_{k})-\alpha}}\right\rvert\geq\tilde{a}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right\rvert
by (
73
), we have

\displaystyle\min_{t\leq k\leq T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq\max_{t\leq k\leq T}(D_{k}/\tilde{a}_{k})+\max_{t\leq k\leq T}(1/\tilde{a}_{k})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma_{J(k)}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})}}\right)^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\epsilon_{t-1,T}|+B+\max_{1\leq j\leq J(T+1)}\gamma_{j}}}\right).

(95)

Now, by (
93
), we have for all
k\in[t,T]
that
q_{T}-q_{k}\leq\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}
, hence

\displaystyle q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}

\displaystyle=q_{T}-q_{k}+q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}+q^{0}_{k,\alpha}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}+|q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}|+|q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}|.

In addition, since
\widetilde{\Pi}_{T}(q_{T})\leq\alpha
, we have
{\Pi}^{0}_{T}(q_{T})\leq\alpha+D_{T}
and
q_{T}\geq q^{0}_{T,\alpha+D_{T}}
, which gives
q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q_{T}\leq q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha+D_{T}}\leq D_{T}/\tilde{a}_{T}
by (
90
).
We hence obtain

\displaystyle|q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}|-\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}}}\right\rvert-\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}|\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min_{t\leq k\leq T}|q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}|}}\right)\wedge\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(D_{T}/\tilde{a}_{T}}}\right).

(96)

Combining (
95
) and (
96
) leads to

\displaystyle|q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}|-\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}}}\right\rvert-\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}|

\displaystyle\leq\max_{t\leq k\leq T}(D_{k}/\tilde{a}_{k})+\max_{t\leq k\leq T}(1/\tilde{a}_{k})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma_{J(k)}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})}}\right)^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(|\epsilon_{t-1,T}|+B+\max_{1\leq j\leq J(T+1)}\gamma_{j}}}\right).

(97)

In case (ii), we have similarly

\displaystyle q_{T+1}-q_{t}

\displaystyle\geq\epsilon_{t-1,T}+\min_{t\leq k\leq T}|\alpha-\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})|\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma_{J(k)}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})

\displaystyle\geq\epsilon_{t-1,T}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\min_{t\leq k\leq T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left\{\tilde{a}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{k}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}}}\right\rvert}}\right\}-\max_{t\leq k\leq T}D_{k}}}\right)\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma_{J(k)}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}),

which also yields (
95
).
Furthermore, for all
k\in[t,T]
,

q_{T}-q_{k}\geq\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}
, hence

q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q_{T}\leq\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}|+\min_{t\leq k\leq T}|q^{0}_{k,\alpha}-q_{k}|+\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}|.

On the other hand, since
\widetilde{\Pi}_{T}(q_{T})\geq\alpha
, we have
{\Pi}^{0}_{T}(q_{T})\geq\alpha-D_{T}
and
q_{T}\leq q^{0}_{T,\alpha-D_{T}}
, which gives
q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}\leq q^{0}_{T,\alpha-D_{T}}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}\leq D_{T}/\tilde{a}_{T}
by (
89
).
This also yields (
96
) and thus also (
97
).
Combining the two cases above gives the bound (by Lemma
B.2
):

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{T}-q^{0}_{T,\alpha}}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\max_{t\leq k\leq T}(D_{k}/\tilde{a}_{k})+\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}|+\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|q^{0}_{T,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}|

\displaystyle+\big(\max_{J(t)\leq j\leq J(T-1)}\gamma_{j}\big)\vee\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\max_{t\leq k\leq T}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(1/\tilde{a}_{k}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}}}\right)^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\epsilon_{t-1,T}}}\right\rvert+B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}}}\right)}}\right]}}\right).

This gives the final bound (
82
).

Let us now prove the last statement and show that the process defined by
(\epsilon_{t,T})_{T\geq t}
(
77
) satisfies (
79
). Define the filtration:

{\mathcal{G}}_{t}=\sigma(\mathcal{F}_{t},S_{t+1}(X_{t+1},q_{t+1})),\>\>t\geq 1.

We first prove that the process
(Z_{t})_{t\geq 1}
defined in (
92
) is a centered martingale with respect to the filtration
({\mathcal{G}}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
with
\mathbb{E}(Z_{T}^{2})\leq\sum_{t=1}^{J(T)}\gamma_{t}^{2}
.
First, for
t\geq 1
,
Z_{t}
is
{\mathcal{F}}_{t}
measurable and thus
{\mathcal{G}}_{t}
measurable, and for
t\geq 2
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Z_{t}-Z_{t-1}\middle|{\mathcal{G}}_{t-1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\gamma_{J(t)}(\mathrm{react}_{t}(Y_{t},{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}),q_{t})-\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(q_{t}))\cdot S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t})\middle|{\mathcal{G}}_{t-1}}}\right]

\displaystyle=0+\gamma_{J(t)}S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t})\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathrm{react}_{t}(Y_{t},{\mathcal{C}}_{t}(X_{t},q_{t}),q_{t})-\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(q_{t})\middle|S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t})=1,{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}}}\right].

Now, the latter is equal to zero by definition of
\widetilde{\Pi}_{t}(q)
and because
q_{t}
is
{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}
measurable.
This means that
\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[Z_{t}\middle|{\mathcal{F}}_{t-1}}}\right]=Z_{t-1}
for
t\geq 2
and thus
(Z_{t})_{t\geq 1}
is a martingale with respect to the filtration
({\mathcal{G}}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
.
Now, this implies that
(\epsilon_{t,T})_{T\geq t}
is also a centered martingale wrt the filtration
({\mathcal{G}}_{T})_{T\geq t}
. In addition, we have for
T>t
,

\Delta\epsilon_{T}:=\epsilon_{t,T}-\epsilon_{t,T-1}=\gamma_{J(T)}(\mathrm{react}_{T}(Y_{T},{\mathcal{C}}_{T}(X_{T},q_{T}))-\widetilde{\Pi}_{T}(q_{T}))\cdot S_{T}(X_{T},q_{T})\in[A_{T},B_{T}],

with
A_{T}=-\widetilde{\Pi}_{T}(q_{T})\gamma_{J(T)}S_{T}(X_{T},q_{T})
and
B_{T}=(1-\widetilde{\Pi}_{T}(q_{T}))\gamma_{J(T)}S_{T}(X_{T},q_{T})
which are both
{\mathcal{G}}_{T-1}
measurable.
In addition, the square compensator is

\displaystyle\langle\epsilon\rangle_{T}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{k=t}^{T}\mathbb{E}[(\Delta\epsilon_{k})^{2}\>|\>{\mathcal{G}}_{T-1}]

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=t}^{T}\mathbb{E}[(\gamma_{J(k)}(\mathrm{react}_{k}(Y_{k},{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}))-\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k}))\cdot S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})))^{2}\>|\>{\mathcal{G}}_{T-1}]

\displaystyle\leq(1/4)\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma_{J(k)}^{2}\cdot S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})=(1/4)\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}^{2},

by using Lemma
B.2
.
Since

\mathcal{D}_{T}=\sum_{k=t}^{T}(B_{k}-A_{k})^{2}\leq\sum_{k=t}^{T}\gamma^{2}_{J(k)}\cdot S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})=\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma^{2}_{j},

by using again Lemma
B.2
.
we have by Lemma
C.10
that
for all
x,y>0
,
T\geq t
,

\mathbb{P}\Big(\epsilon_{t,T}>x,\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}^{2}\leq 2y/3\Big)\leq e^{-3x^{2}/y}.

Lemma B.2
.

For any function
\Psi(\cdot)
, for all
T\geq 1
, we have

\sum_{k=1}^{T}\Psi(\gamma_{J(k)})S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})=\sum_{j=1}^{J(T+1)-1}\Psi(\gamma_{j}).

(98)

Proof.

Denote for all
j\geq 1
,

\tau_{j}=\min\{k\geq 1\>:\>J(k+1)=j+1\}=\min\Big\{k\geq 1\>:\>\sum_{i=1}^{k}S_{i}(X_{i},q_{i})=j\Big\}

the first time
k
for which there have been
j
selections at times
\leq k
.
Note that
J(\tau_{j}+1)=j+1
,
J(\tau_{j})=j
and
S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})=1
for
k=\tau_{j}
.
Hence, for any function
\Psi(\cdot)
, for all
T\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{T}\Psi(\gamma_{J(k)})S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 1}\Psi(\gamma_{J(\tau_{j})})\sum_{k=1}^{T}{\mathbf{1}\{k=\tau_{j}\}}S_{k}(X_{k},q_{k})

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 1}\Psi(\gamma_{j})\sum_{k=1}^{T}{\mathbf{1}\{k=\tau_{j}\}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j\geq 1}\Psi(\gamma_{j}){\mathbf{1}\{\tau_{j}\leq T\}}.

Now,
\tau_{j}\leq T
if and only if
J(T+1)\geq j+1
. This gives (
98
).
∎

B.3
Proof of Theorem
3.1

As proven in §
B.2.2
, the process
(Z_{t})_{t\geq 1}
defined in (
92
) is a centered martingale with respect to the filtration
({\mathcal{G}}_{t})_{t\geq 1}
with
\sup_{T\geq 1}\mathbb{E}(Z_{T}^{2})\leq\sum_{t=1}^{+\infty}\gamma_{t}^{2}<+\infty
by assumption.
Therefore, by Doob’s martingale convergence theorem, there exists some random variable
Z
such that
Z_{t}\to Z
when
t
tends to infinity, almost surely. This means that, almost surely,
Z_{t}
is a Cauchy sequence.

Recall now the bound (
78
), proved in Theorem
A.2
: for all
T>t\geq 1
, we have point-wise

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{T}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq\>

\displaystyle(\max_{t\leq k\leq T}D_{k})/\tilde{a}+\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}|+\gamma_{J(t)}+\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\tilde{a}\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}}}\right)^{-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\epsilon_{t-1,T}}}\right\rvert+B+\gamma_{1}}}\right).

Fix now
\varepsilon>0
and assume that we are on the event
\{\lim_{t\to\infty}J(t)=+\infty\}\cap\{\lim_{t\to\infty}D_{t}=0\}
. Since
Z_{t}
is a Cauchy sequence (almost surely) then there exist
T_{1}=T_{1}(\varepsilon)
such that for all
T\geq t\geq T_{1}
we have

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|\epsilon_{t-1,T}}}\right\rvert\vee\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|\epsilon_{k-1,T-1}|=(Z_{T}-Z_{t-1})\vee\max_{t\leq k\leq T-1}|Z_{T-1}-Z_{k-1}|\leq\varepsilon.

Since
\lim_{j\to\infty}\gamma_{j}=0
, there exists
J_{1}=J_{1}(\varepsilon)
such that for
J\geq J_{1}
,
\gamma_{J}\leq\varepsilon
. Also, since
\lim_{t\to\infty}J(t)=+\infty
, there exists
T_{2}=T_{2}(\varepsilon)
such that for all
t\geq T_{2}
,
J(t)\geq J_{1}
and thus
\gamma_{J(t)}\leq\varepsilon
.
Furthermore, since
\lim_{t\to\infty}D_{t}=0
, there exists
T_{3}=T_{3}(\varepsilon)
such that for all
T\geq t\geq T_{3}
,
\max_{t\leq k\leq T}D_{k}\leq\tilde{a}\>\varepsilon
.

Now consider
t=\max(T_{1},T_{2},T_{3})
. Since
\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}
tends to infinity when
T
tends to infinity, there exists
T_{4}=T_{4}(\varepsilon)\geq t
such that for
T\geq T_{4}
,

\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\sum_{j=J(t)}^{J(T+1)-1}\gamma_{j}}}\right)^{-1}\leq\frac{\tilde{a}}{\varepsilon+B+\gamma_{1}}\varepsilon.

This provides that for any
T\geq T_{4}
, we have
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{T}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq 4\varepsilon
and concludes the proof.

B.4
Proof of Theorem
A.3

Similarly to §
B.2.2
, we show a version of Theorem
A.3
which is slightly more general, accommodating the case where
\Pi^{0}_{t}
may depend on time.

B.4.1
Extending Theorem
A.3

Theorem B.3
.

Consider the
OnlineSCI
threshold sequence
(q_{t}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}})_{t\geq 1}
with any starting point
q_{1}^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}\in[0,B]
and any step sequence
(\gamma_{j})_{j\geq 1}
. For each
t\geq 1
, consider some function
\Pi^{0}_{t}
satisfying Assumption
7
with some
q^{0}_{t,\alpha}\in(0,B)
and the corresponding
D_{t}
in (
20
).
Let
a_{t}
and
\tilde{a}_{t}
being defined by (
73
) and (
75
), respectively, in which
\Pi^{0}(q)
and
q^{0}_{\alpha}
have been replaced by
\Pi^{0}_{t}(q)
and
q^{0}_{t,\alpha}
, respectively.
Let

\tilde{a}=\inf_{t\geq 1}\tilde{a}_{t}\geq 0
.
Assume that for some
t\geq j_{0}\geq 1
,

•

(\gamma_{j})_{j_{0}\leq j\leq t}
is nonincreasing,
1-2\tilde{a}\gamma_{j_{0}}\geq 0
and
\gamma_{j}/\gamma_{j+1}\leq 1/(1-\tilde{a}\gamma_{j})
for
j_{0}\leq j\leq t
;

•

S_{k}
is independent of any
\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
-measurable variable, for
j_{0}\leq k\leq t
.

Then, for any
t_{0}
with
j_{0}\leq t_{0}\leq t
with
J(t_{0})\geq j_{0}
, we have pointwise

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}-q^{0}_{t,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1}\dots S_{t-1}}}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\gamma_{J(t)}}{\gamma_{J(t_{0})}}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})^{2}\vee\frac{4}{\tilde{a}}}}\right)+4(B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})\gamma_{J(t)}\sum_{k=t_{0}}^{t-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle+4\gamma_{J(t)}\sum_{k=t_{0}}^{t-1}S_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[D_{k}\mid S_{1}\dots S_{k-1}}}\right]-\gamma_{J(k)}}}\right)_{+},

(99)

where we denoted
S_{t}=S_{t}(X_{t},q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t})
. Note that the bound (
99
) is a bit better than (
80
), because of the “
-\gamma_{J(k)}
” term in the last sum of the right-hand-side.

Theorem
B.3
is proved in §
B.4.2
.

B.4.2
Proof of Theorem
B.3

In this proof, we denote
q^{{\mbox{{\tiny SCI}}}}_{t}
by
q_{t}
and
S_{t}(X_{t},q_{t})
by
S_{t}
for short.
We also denote
S_{1:i}
for the sequence
S_{1},\dots,S_{i}
.
We have for all
t\geq t_{0}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{t}-q^{0}_{t,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:{t-1}}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(t)}}\vee\frac{4}{\tilde{a}}-\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{t_{0}}-q^{0}_{t_{0},\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:{t_{0}-1}}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(t_{0})}}\vee\frac{4}{\tilde{a}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k=t_{0}}^{t-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k+1}-q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:{k}}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k+1)}}\vee\frac{4}{\tilde{a}}-\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:{k-1}}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k)}}\vee\frac{4}{\tilde{a}}}}\right).

We now use the following relation (see below for a proof): for
k\geq t_{0}
such that
J(k)\geq j_{0}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k+1}-q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:k}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k+1)}}\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:k-1}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k)}}\vee\frac{4}{\tilde{a}}

\displaystyle+4(B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right\rvert+4S_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[D_{k}\mid S_{1:k-1}}}\right]-\gamma_{J(k)}}}\right)_{+}.

(100)

Putting this relation into the above display provides that, provided that
J(t_{0})\geq j_{0}
, for all
t\geq t_{0}
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{t}-q^{0}_{t,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:{t-1}}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(t)}}\vee\frac{4}{\tilde{a}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})^{2}}{\gamma_{J(t_{0})}}\vee\frac{4}{\tilde{a}}+4\sum_{k=t_{0}}^{t-1}S_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left((B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[D_{k}\mid S_{1:k-1}}}\right]-\gamma_{J(k)}}}\right)_{+}+4(B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})\sum_{k=t_{0}}^{t-1}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right\rvert,

which proves the result.

Let us now finally prove (
100
).
To show this, first observe that by definition

\displaystyle\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k+1}-q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}+q^{0}_{k,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}+\gamma_{J(k)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{react}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{k},{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}),q_{k}}}\right)-\alpha}}\right)S_{k}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}+2\gamma_{J(k)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{react}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{k},{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}),q_{k}}}\right)-\alpha}}\right)S_{k}

\displaystyle+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{0}_{k,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}}}\right)+2\gamma_{J(k)}^{2}S_{k}+2\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q^{0}_{k,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}+2\gamma_{J(k)}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(\mathrm{react}_{k}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(Y_{k},{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(X_{k},q_{k}),q_{k}}}\right)-\alpha}}\right)S_{k}

\displaystyle+2\gamma_{J(k)}^{2}S_{k}+4(B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right\rvert.

Denote for
q\in\mathbb{R}
,
\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q)=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{react}_{k}(Y_{k},{\mathcal{C}}_{k}(X_{k},q))\>|\>\mathcal{F}_{k-1},S_{k}=1]=\Pi_{k}(q)\cdot{\mathbf{1}\{q\geq 0\}}+{\mathbf{1}\{q<0\}}
, where
\Pi_{k}(q):=\Pi_{{\mathcal{C}}_{k},S_{k}}(q)
is given by (
18
).
We thus obtain

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k+1}-q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:k}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k+1)}}-4(B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j})\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q^{0}_{k+1,\alpha}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:k}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k+1)}}+\frac{2\gamma_{J(k)}^{2}}{\gamma_{J(k+1)}}S_{k}+\frac{2\gamma_{J(k)}}{\gamma_{J(k)+1}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\frac{\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})-\alpha}{q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}\mid S_{1},\dots,S_{k}}}\right]S_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:k}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k+1)}}+\frac{2\gamma_{J(k)}^{2}}{\gamma_{J(k+1)}}S_{k}+\frac{2\gamma_{J(k)}}{\gamma_{J(k)+1}}\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\frac{\widetilde{\Pi}_{k}(q_{k})-\alpha}{q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}\mid S_{1:k-1}}}\right]S_{k},

(101)

where we used in the last step that by assumption
S_{k}
is independent of the
\mathcal{F}_{k-1}
-measurable variable
q_{k}
.
Similarly, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:k}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k+1)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:k}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k)}}(1-S_{k})+\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:k}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k)+1}}S_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:k-1}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k)}}(1-S_{k})+\frac{\mathbb{E}\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left[\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left(q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right)^{2}\mid S_{1:k-1}}}\right]}{\gamma_{J(k)+1}}S_{k},

In addition, by Assumptions
7
and
8
, and
since
-\alpha\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}\leq q_{k}\leq B+(1-\alpha)\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}
and
\mathopen{}\mathclose{{\left|q_{k}-q^{0}_{k,\alpha}}}\right\rvert\leq B+\sup_{j\geq 1}\gamma_{j}
almost surely by Lemma
2.1
, we derive by using (
86
) and (
85
) that
